451.29065pt}$}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.44456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(\zeta)e^{ir\cdot\zeta}d\zeta\right)dr

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\int_{[-\pi m,\pi m]^{2}}\overline{v(r)}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}a(r,\zeta)u(-\zeta)e^{ir\cdot\zeta}d\zeta\right)dr

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(-\zeta)\overline{\left(\int_{[-\pi m,\pi m]^{2}}v(r)\overline{a(r,\zeta)}e^{-ir\cdot\zeta}dr\right)}d\zeta.

Here,
\mathcal{F}
represents the Fourier transform in the sense of distributions.
Due to Plancherel’s theorem and the fact that
v(r)\overline{a(r,\xi)}
is
2\pi
-periodic on the
r
variable,

\displaystyle\int_{[-\pi m,\pi m]^{2}}v(r)\overline{a(r,\zeta)}e^{-ir\cdot\zeta}dr=

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}v(r)\overline{a(r,\zeta)}e^{-i\xi\cdot r}dr\right)\cdot\left(\int_{[-m\pi,m\pi]^{2}}e^{ir\cdot(\xi-\zeta)}dr\right).

Plugging it into the previous expression,

\displaystyle\langle\text{Op}(a)^{\star}v,\,u\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{2}}\overline{v(r)}a(r,\zeta)e^{i\xi\cdot r}dr\right)\left(\int_{[-\pi m,\pi m]^{2}}e^{i(\zeta-\xi)\cdot r}dr\right)u(-\zeta)d\zeta.

We are now interested in taking the integral over
[-\pi m,\pi m]^{2}
outside to be able to take the limit. We see that, for every
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\overline{v(r)}a(r,\zeta)\in C^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\,\text{ and }\,\|\overline{v}(\cdot)a(\cdot,\zeta)\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq C,

with
C
independent of
\zeta
, so by means of Lemma
2.9
, the Fourier coefficients

c(\xi,\zeta)=\int\overline{v(r)}a(r,\zeta)e^{i\xi\cdot x}\ dx\in A(\mathbb{T}).

Moreover,

\left\lVert c(\xi,\zeta)\right\rVert_{\ell^{1}_{\xi}}\leq C\left\lVert\overline{v(\cdot)}a(\cdot,\zeta)\right\rVert_{k,\alpha}\leq C,

with
C
independent of
\zeta
. Therefore,

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{[-\pi m,\pi m]^{2}}&\left|\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{2}}\overline{v(r)}a(r,\zeta)e^{i\xi\cdot r}dr\right)e^{i(\zeta-\xi)\cdot x}u(\zeta)\right|\,dx\ d\zeta\\
&\leq C(2\pi m)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|u(\zeta)|d\zeta<\infty.\end{split}

Invoking Fubini’s theorem, we can exchange the order of the
x
, the
\zeta
and the
\xi
integrals, where the latter is taken with respect to the counting measure. Hence,

(2.12)

\langle\text{Op}(a)^{\star}v,\,u\rangle=\lim_{m\longrightarrow\infty}\int_{[-\pi m,\pi m]^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{2}}v(r)\overline{a(r,\zeta)}e^{-i\xi\cdot r}dr\right)e^{i(\zeta-\xi)\cdot x}u(\zeta)d\zeta\right)dx.

To conclude the proof we would like to check that the limit tends to the integral in the whole space, but it is not even clear whether such limit even exists. To show the existence of the limit, we have to provide a better decay for the integrand in the
x
variable via an oscillatory integral type argument. More precisely, we have that

\displaystyle(1+|x|^{2})

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{2}}\overline{v(r)}a(r,\zeta)e^{i\xi\cdot r}dr\right)e^{i(-\zeta+\xi)\cdot x}u(\zeta)d\zeta\right)dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{2}}\overline{v(r)}a(r,\zeta)e^{i\xi\cdot r}dr\right)(1-\Delta_{\zeta})e^{i(\zeta-\xi)\cdot x}u(\zeta)d\zeta\right)dx.

Using integration by parts combined with the fact that
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(and therefore has very fast decay) we get a combination of terms of the following type

\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{2}}v(r)\partial^{\alpha}_{\zeta}\overline{a(r,\zeta)}e^{-i\xi\cdot r}dr\right)e^{i(\zeta-\xi)\cdot x}\partial^{\beta}_{\zeta}u(\zeta)d\zeta\right)dx.

Consequently, since
a\in S^{0}(1+\alpha)
,we can invoke Proposition
2.9
once again, to show that the resulting integral is finite. This fact, allows to take the limit in
m
and also to exchange the
x
integral and the sum in (
2.12
) to find that

\langle\text{Op}(a)^{\star}v,\,u\rangle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{2}}\overline{v(r)}a(r,\zeta)e^{i\xi\cdot r}dr\right)e^{i(\zeta-\xi)\cdot x}u(\zeta)d\zeta\right)dx.

Finally, notice that the function

\zeta\mapsto u(-\zeta)\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{2}}\overline{v(r)}a(r,\zeta)e^{i\xi\cdot r}dr

belongs to the Schwarz class and hence by the Fourier inversion formula, it follows that

\langle\text{Op}(a)^{\star}v,\,u\rangle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{2}}\overline{v(r)}a(r,\xi)e^{i\xi\cdot r}dr\right)u(-\xi).

Now, since that for any
v\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, it holds that

\langle v,\widehat{u}\rangle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\overline{\widehat{v}(\xi)}u(-\xi),

the result follows.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

Theorem
2.10
shows that if
a\in S^{m}(1+\alpha)
, we can define
\text{Op}(a)^{\star}v
for
v\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, and obtain formula (
2.11
) for its Fourier coefficients. One can notice that such expression only depends on the values of
a(x,\xi)
for
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. This implies that
\text{Op}(a)^{\star}v=\text{Op}(b)^{\star}v
for every other symbol
b\in S^{m}(1+\alpha)
that coincides with
a(x,\xi)
on
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

In this article the situation in somehow reversed, i.e. we are given a certain operator
T
acting on
C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
that formally resembles the adjoint of a pseudodifferential operator with a symbol
\tilde{a}(x,\xi)
that is a priori only defined for
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Thus, we can invoke Theorem
2.10
if we extend the symbol
\widetilde{a}(x,\xi)
to all
\mathbb{R}^{2}
smoothly so that the extension
a(x,\xi)\in S^{m}(1+\alpha)
. Typically, there is one obvious extension of
\widetilde{a}(x,\xi)
in such a way that the new symbol
a(x,\xi)
is smooth outside the origin and satisfies

(2.13)

\|\partial_{\xi}^{\gamma}a(\cdot,\xi)\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C_{\gamma}|\xi|^{m-|\gamma|}.

However, extending the symbol is such a way lacks the correct behavior at the origin. Nevertheless, taking a cut-off function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\phi\equiv 1
on small neighborhood around the origin and
\phi\equiv 0
outside a ball of radius
\frac{1}{2}
, the symbol

S^{m}(1+\alpha)\ni b(x,\xi)=\varphi(0)\cdot\tilde{a}(x,0)+(1-\varphi(\xi))a(x,\xi)),

and extends
\tilde{a}(x,\xi)
. As a consequence, due to Theorem
2.10
, the operator
T
defines a bounded operator in
C^{1,\alpha}
and its norm can be estimated by the constants
C_{\alpha}
in (
2.13
).

2.3.1.
Classical Mikhlin-Hörmander multipliers in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{T}^{d}

A particular case of pseudodifferential operators are Mikhlin-Hörmander multipliers. Mikhlin-Hörmander multipliers of degree
m
are “pseudodifferential operators” with symbols
a
that only depend on
\xi
and that satisfy a modified version of the decay estimates:

|\partial^{\gamma}_{\xi}a(\xi)|\leq C_{\gamma}|\xi|^{m-|\gamma|},\ \forall\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}.

These can be defined for a large class of functions, and some multiplier theory give us boundedness in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for instance. Note that, due to the Remark
2.11
, Mihklin-Hörmander multipliers are bounded from
C^{m+\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to
C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and more in general

C^{k+m,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to
C^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for
k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, cf.
[
38
]
.

There are two prominent examples operators whose symbols are Mihklin-Hörmander multipliers: the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}
and the Riesz operator
\mathcal{R}_{j}
. The former is given by the principal value integral

\mathcal{H}f(x)=\mbox{p.v.}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-y)\ \frac{dy}{y},

and has the associated multiplier

m(\xi)=-i\text{sgn}(\xi),\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

The latter is also expressed by the principal value integral

\mathcal{R}_{j}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{y_{j}}{|y|^{d+1}}f(x-y)\ dy,

where
j=1,\ldots,d.
The multiplier is given by

m_{j}(\xi)=-i\frac{\xi_{j}}{|\xi|}.

We take as a convention that
m_{j}(0)=0
.
The Riesz transform operator will appear throughout this work repeatedly. Moreover, as a consequence of the previous exposed arguments we infer that the Riesz transform is a bounded linear operator such that

(2.14)

\mathcal{R}_{j}:C^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to C^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

2.4.
Hölder estimates for the div-curl system and transport type problem

In this subsection, we present two auxiliary results regarding Hölder estimates for the two building blocks, the div-curl and transport problem, employed in order to apply the so called vorticity-transport method mentioned in Section
1
. Although the two auxiliary results rely on well-known and classical ideas (cf.
[
17
,
20
]
), we prefer to include them in this subsection to make the article as self-contained as possible.

2.4.1.
Hölder estimates for the div-curl system.

The first auxiliary result we present deals with the well-posedness and regularity of solutions to the div-curl system. More precisely, we show the following result:

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]
,
j\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
f\in C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)
, and
J_{1}
,
J_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume that
j
is divergence free, and that that
f
and
j_{3}
satisfy the following compatibility condition

(2.15)

\int_{\partial\Omega_{-}}j_{3}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\partial\Omega_{-}}f=\int_{\partial\Omega_{+}}f.

Then, there exists a unique solution
W\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3})
for the div-curl problem

(2.16)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\nabla\times W=j&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\nabla\cdot W=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
W\cdot n=f&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\\
\end{array}\right.

with
\int_{\{x=0\}}Wd\vec{S}=J_{1},\quad\int_{\{y=0\}}Wd\vec{S}=J_{2}
. Furthermore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(2.17)

\|W\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\|j\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)}+|J_{1}|+|J_{2}|\right).

Proof of Proposition
2.12
.

The proof follows the lines in
[
3
, Proposition 3.11]
. However, we need to modify the argument accordingly to the three-dimensional setting. To solve the system (
2.16
) we first examine two complementary auxiliary problems, namely

(2.18)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\Delta Z=-j&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
Z_{1}=0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega,\\
Z_{2}=0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega,\\
\partial_{3}Z_{3}=0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

and

(2.19)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\Delta\phi=0&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{\nu}\phi=f&\text{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

Here,
Z=(Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3})
is a vector,
\phi
is a scalar function and
\nu
is the outward unit normal vector to
\partial\Omega
and
\partial_{\nu}\phi=\nabla\phi\cdot\nu
. Therefore, since
j\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the components

Z_{1},Z_{2}
are the unique solutions to the classical Dirichlet problem. Moreover, by classical Hölder estimates it is well-known that

(2.20)

\|Z_{1}\|_{C^{3,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\|j\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)},\quad\|Z_{2}\|_{C^{3,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\|j\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)}.

On the other hand, due to Schauder theory for the Neumann problem and the compatibility condition (
2.15
) there exists a unique zero mean solution
\phi
to (
2.19
). Moreover, we have that

(2.21)

\|\phi\|_{C^{3,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)}.

We are left with the existence and uniqueness of the component
Z_{3}
which solves a Neumann type problem. Therefore, to ensure the existence and uniqueness we just need to show that

\int_{\Omega}j_{3}=0.

Indeed, we find that

\frac{d}{dz}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}j_{3}\,dxdy=-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\partial_{1}j_{1}dxdy-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\partial_{2}j_{2}\,dxdy=0,

where we have used the divergence free condition on
j
and the periodicity of
j
in the
x,y
variables. Thus, using the compatibility condition (
2.15
) on
\partial\Omega_{-}
we conclude the assertion. Consequently, a unique mean zero
Z_{3}
solution exists. Moreover, similarly as before, it satisfies

(2.22)

\|Z_{3}\|_{C^{3,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\|j\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)}.

Combining (
2.20
) and (
2.22
) we infer that

(2.23)

\|Z\|_{C^{3,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\|j\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)}.

To conclude we define
W
as

W=\nabla\times Z+\nabla\phi+A_{1}\vec{e}_{1}+A_{2}\vec{e}_{2},

where
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are given by

(2.24)

\displaystyle A_{1}=\frac{J_{1}}{2\pi L}-\int_{0}^{L}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\nabla\times Z(0,y,z)\cdot e_{1}dydz-\int_{0}^{L}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\nabla\phi(0,y,z)\cdot e_{1}dydz,

(2.25)

\displaystyle A_{2}=\frac{J_{2}}{2\pi L}-\int_{0}^{L}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\nabla\times Z(x,0,z)\cdot e_{2}dxdz-\int_{0}^{L}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\nabla\phi(x,0,z)\cdot e_{2}dxdz.

Therefore, it can be readily check that

\nabla\times W=\nabla\times\nabla\times Z=\nabla(\nabla\cdot Z)-\Delta Z=j\quad\mbox{in }\Omega.

Notice that
\nabla\cdot Z=0
, since
Z\in C^{3,\alpha}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3})
solves (
2.18
) and by hypothesis
\nabla\cdot j=0.
Similarly, we have that
\nabla\cdot W=\Delta\phi=0
in
\Omega
and
W\cdot n=(\nabla\times Z)\cdot n+\partial_{n}\phi=f
on
\partial\Omega.
Moreover, by construction of the constants
A_{1},A_{2}
the flux condition on
W
are also satisfied. Furthermore, by means of (
2.21
) and (
2.23
) we find that

(2.26)

\|W\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\|j\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)}+|J_{1}|+|J_{2}|\right).

To show the uniqueness of solutions, assume that
W\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3})
solution to (
2.16
) with
f=j=J_{1}=J_{2}=0
. Then, we have that

\nabla\times\left(\nabla\times W\right)=-\Delta W=0\mbox{ in }\Omega.

Since
W_{3}=0
on
\partial\Omega
, we conclude that
W_{3}=0
in
\Omega
. Hence,

\nabla\times W=(-\partial_{3}W_{2},\partial_{3}W_{1},\partial_{1}W_{2}-\partial_{2}W_{1})=0,

i.e.
W_{i}=W_{i}(x,y)
for
i=1,2
are harmonic functions in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. Thus,

W=(A_{1},A_{2},0)\mbox{ in }\Omega,

for
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. However, since
J_{1}=J_{2}=0
we obtain that
W\equiv 0
in
\Omega
. Then uniqueness follows by linearity.
∎

2.4.2.
Hölder estimates for the transport problem

The second auxiliary result focuses on Hölder estimates for a transport problem. We present first an elementary result regarding the uniqueness and regularity for a system of ODEs, using the method of characteristics. We just sketch the proof, since it is a straightforward adaption to the three dimensional setting of the result in
[
3
, Proposition 3.7]
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
v\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then, there exists a unique solution
\Psi_{z}\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)
solving the system of ODEs

(2.27)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\partial\Psi_{z}}{\partial z}=v(\Psi_{z},z),\quad z\in[0,L]\\
\Psi_{0}=(x,y),\quad(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}\end{array}\right..

Moreover, for every
z
,
\Psi_{z}
describes a
C^{2,\alpha}
embedding, so that there exists a constant
C=C\left(\|v\|_{2,\alpha},L\right)
such that

(2.28)

\|\Psi_{z}\|_{2,\alpha}\leq C\qquad\|\Psi^{-1}_{z}\|_{2,\alpha}\leq C.

In order to show Proposition
2.13
we will repeatedly use the following calculus result

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
u:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
be a Lipschitz function, and
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
C^{\alpha}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d})
function. Then,
f\circ u\in C^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof of Proposition
2.13
.

The existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
2.27
) follows from the classical Picard-Lindelöf theorem. Moreover, since
v
is a bounded, periodic and globally Lipschitz, the solution exists for all
z\in[0,L]
. The the function
\Psi_{z}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\rightarrow\Omega
defines a topological embedding for each
z
(cf.
[
12
, Theorem 7.1]
). In only remains to check the regularity bounds (
2.28
). Direct differentiation shows that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{z}}{\partial z}

\displaystyle=v(\Psi_{z},z),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\Psi_{z}}{\partial z^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial v(\Psi_{z},z)}{\partial X}v_{1}(\Psi_{z},z)+\frac{\partial v(\Psi_{z},z)}{\partial Y}v_{2}(\Psi_{z},z)+\frac{\partial v(\Psi_{z},z)}{\partial z}.

Therefore, invoking Lemma
2.14
we find that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{z}\Psi_{z}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|v\|_{\infty},\quad\|\partial^{2}_{z}\Psi_{z}\|_{\infty}\leq C\|Dv\|_{\infty}(\|v\|_{\infty}+1),

\displaystyle[\partial^{2}_{z}\Psi_{z}]_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq C([Dv]_{\alpha}\|v\|_{\infty}+\|Dv\|_{\infty}[v]_{\alpha}+[Dv]_{\alpha})(\|D\Psi_{z}\|_{\infty}^{\alpha}+1).

Since
[Dv]_{\alpha}\leq C\|v\|_{C^{2}}
, we get the desired estimates for the
z
derivatives of
\Psi_{z}
. To bound the
x
derivatives, standard results of differentiability with respect to parameters of ordinary differential equations yield

\left|\frac{\partial\Psi_{z}}{\partial x}\right|\leq\left(1+\|v\|_{C^{1}}Le^{L\|v\|_{C^{1}}}\right),

and hence we obtain bounds for the
C^{1}
norm of
\Psi_{z}.
To estimate the
C^{\alpha}
Hölder semi-norm in the
x
variable and higher order
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates we proceed in similar fashion.
In order to show
C^{2,\alpha}
bounds for the inverse
\Psi_{z}^{-1}
, we find the following relation between

\Psi_{z}=(X(x,y,z),Y(x,y,z)),\quad\Psi^{-1}_{z}=(X^{-1}(x,y,z),Y^{-1}(x,y,z)),

namely,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\partial X^{-1}}{\partial x}&\frac{\partial X^{-1}}{\partial y}\\
\\
\frac{\partial Y^{-1}}{\partial x}&\frac{\partial Y^{-1}}{\partial y}\end{array}\right)(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{z}D\,v(x,y,s)ds\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\partial Y}{\partial y}&-\frac{\partial X}{\partial y}\\
\\
-\frac{\partial Y}{\partial x}&\frac{\partial X}{\partial x}\end{array}\right)(\Psi_{z}^{-1}(x,y),z).

Using the previous relation we can readily check that
\Psi_{z}^{-1}
is Lipschitz. Moreover, using the representation one can compute Hölder semi-norms and higher order derivatives in terms of
\Psi_{z}
, proving the desired bounds (
2.28
) and concluding the proof.
∎

In the following we will derive Hölder estimates for the hyperbolic transport problem

(2.29)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{3}j+(b\cdot\nabla)j=(j\cdot\nabla)b&\text{in }\Omega,\\
j=j_{0}&\text{on }\partial\Omega_{-}.\end{array}\right.

More precisely, we show the following result:

Proposition 2.15
.

There exists
M_{0}
small enough so that for every
b\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq M<M_{0}
and
j_{0}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the system (
2.29
) admits a unique solution
j\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Moreover, there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,L)
such that

(2.30)

\|j\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}.

Furthermore, let
j^{1},j^{2}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
be two solutions to (
2.29
) with
b
given by
b^{1},b^{2}
respectively. Then, we have the lower order estimate

(2.31)

\|j^{1}-j^{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\leq C\left[\|j_{0}^{1}-j_{0}^{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}}+\|j_{0}^{1}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\|b^{1}-b^{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\right].

Remark 2.16
.

If we compare Proposition
2.15
with its two-dimensional analogue shown in
[
3
, Proposition 3.8]
,
[
4
, Proposition 12]
, we notice that in this case we have an extra stretching term in the hyperbolic equation (
2.29
), namely,
(j\cdot\nabla)b
. Nevertheless, from the regularity point of view, this term has better regularity than the transport term, and thus we can show identical bounds (
2.30
)-(
2.31
) as for its two-dimensional analogue. The idea of the proof also follow closely the one in
[
3
, Proposition 3.8]
,
[
4
, Proposition 12]
.

Proof of Proposition
2.15
.

It is well-known that the first order hyperbolic transport problem (
2.29
) can be solved via method of characteristics. More precisely, let us introduce the flow map defined by (characteristic lines of (
2.29
))

(2.32)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\partial X}{\partial z}&=\frac{b_{1}}{1+b_{3}},\\
\frac{\partial Y}{\partial z}&=\frac{b_{2}}{1+b_{3}},\end{array}\right.

with initial data
X(x,y,0)=x,\,Y(x,y,0)=y
. Here, we denoted by
b_{\ell}
for
\ell=1,2,3
, the components of the vector field
b
.
System (
2.32
) yields formally two maps

(2.33)

\Psi_{z}=(X,Y),\quad\Phi=(\Psi_{z},z).

Moreover, note that if
M_{0}<1
, the right hand side in (
2.32
)

\frac{b_{1}}{1+b_{3}}\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega),\quad\frac{b_{2}}{1+b_{3}}\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega),

and hence, Proposition
2.13
shows that
\Psi_{z},\Phi\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)
with
C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)
inverse. Therefore, the solution
j
to system (
2.29
) is explicitly given by

(2.34)

j(x,y,z)=w\circ\Phi^{-1}(x,y,z),

where
w
satisfies the differential equation

(2.35)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\partial w}{\partial z}=A(b(\Phi))\cdot w&0\leq z\leq L\\
w=j_{0}&z=0\end{array}\right.,

where the matrix
A(b)
has entries
A_{ij}=\frac{\partial_{j}b_{i}}{1+b_{3}}
with
i,j=1,2,3.
We know that
\Phi
is Lipschitz, and thus (again, since

M_{0}<1
) we can deduce that the function
A(b(\Phi))\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3},M_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
. Therefore, the system (
2.35
) has
a unique solution
w
for every
(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
. As a consequence, the representation formula (
2.34
) gives us the unique solution for the hyperbolic transport equation (
2.29
).

In order to prove estimates (
2.30
) and (
2.31
) we make use of the
C^{2,\alpha}
regularity of
\Phi
and the fact that
w
satisfies a linear equation. Integrating (
2.35
) and using Grönwall’s inequality we find that

\|w\|_{\infty}\leq\|j_{0}\|_{\infty}\left(1+\frac{\|b\|_{C^{1}}}{1-M_{0}}L\exp\left(\frac{L\|b\|_{C^{1}}}{1-M_{0}}\right)\right),

and hence by means of the representation formula (
2.34
) we deduce that

\|j\|_{\infty}\leq C\|j_{0}\|_{\infty}.

To derive higher order estimates, we make use of the classical chain rule formula

(2.36)

Dj=\left(Dw\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)\cdot D\Phi^{-1}.

One the one hand, recalling Proposition
2.13
we infer that
D\Phi^{-1}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)
, and thus it is Lipschitz. On the other hand, direct use of the representation formulas (
2.34
) and (
2.35
) shows that

\left\|\frac{\partial w}{\partial z}\right\|_{\infty}\leq C\|b\|_{C^{1}}\frac{1}{1-M_{0}}\|w\|_{\infty}\leq C\|j_{0}\|_{\infty}.

Similarly, differentiating w.r.t the
x
variable in (
2.35
) we obtain

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial x}=\frac{\partial A_{ij}}{\partial X}\frac{\partial X}{\partial x}w_{j}+\frac{\partial A_{ij}}{\partial Y}\frac{\partial Y}{\partial x}w_{j}+A_{ij}\frac{\partial w_{j}}{\partial x}.

Integrating for
0\leq z\leq L

and invoking once again Grönwalls inequality we find that

\left\|\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left(\|j\|_{C^{1}}+C\|b\|_{C^{2}}\|\Phi\|_{C^{1}}\|w\|_{\infty}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{1-M_{0}}\|b\|_{C^{1}}Le^{\frac{1}{1-M_{0}}\|b\|_{C^{1}}}\right)\leq C\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1}}.

Mimicking the previous estimates w.r.t the
y
variable and combining them together shows that
\|w\|_{C^{1}}\leq C\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1}}
. Hence, using formula (
2.36
) we conclude that

\|j\|_{C^{1}}\leq C\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1}}.

Hence, since
\|D\Phi^{-1}\|_{\alpha}\leq C
, estimate (
2.30
) follows if we prove that
[Dw]_{\alpha}\leq C\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}
. To that purpose, denoting by
r_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1}),\,r_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2})
and differentiating (
2.35
) we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial x}(r_{1},z)-\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial x}(r_{2},z)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left.\sum_{j}\nabla A_{ij}\frac{\partial\Psi_{s}}{\partial x}w_{j}\right|_{(r_{1},z)}-\left.\sum_{j}\nabla A_{ij}\frac{\partial\Psi_{s}}{\partial x}w_{j}\right|_{(r_{2},z)}

\displaystyle+\left.\sum_{j}A_{ij}\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial x}\right|_{(r_{1},z)}-\left.\sum_{j}A_{ij}\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial x}\right|_{(r_{2},z)}.

Integrating between
0
and
z
and suitably pairing the terms, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial x}(r_{1},z)-\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial x}(r_{2},z)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{\partial j_{0}}{\partial x}(r_{1})-\frac{\partial j_{0}}{\partial x}(r_{2})\right|

\displaystyle+\sum_{j}\int_{0}^{z}\left|\nabla A_{ij}(r_{1},s)-\nabla A_{ij}(r_{2},s)\right|\left|\frac{\partial\Psi_{s}}{\partial x}(r_{1})\right|\left|w_{j}(r_{1},s)\right|ds

\displaystyle+\sum_{j}\int_{0}^{z}\left|\nabla A_{ij}(r_{2},s)\right|\left|\frac{\partial\Psi_{s}}{\partial x}(r_{1})-\frac{\partial\Psi_{s}}{\partial x}(r_{2})\right|\left|w_{j}(r_{1},s)\right|ds

\displaystyle+\sum_{j}\int_{0}^{z}\left|\nabla A_{ij}(r_{2},s)\right|\left|\frac{\partial\Psi_{s}}{\partial x}(r_{2})\right|\left|w_{j}(r_{1},s)-w_{j}(r_{2},s)\right|ds

\displaystyle+\sum_{j}\int_{0}^{z}\left|A_{ij}(r_{1},s)-A_{ij}(r_{2},s)\right|\left|\frac{\partial w_{j}}{\partial x}(r_{1},s)\right|ds

\displaystyle+\sum_{j}\int_{0}^{z}\left|A_{ij}(r_{2},s)\right|\left|\frac{\partial w_{j}}{\partial x}(r_{1},s)-\frac{\partial w_{j}}{\partial x}(r,s)\right|ds

\displaystyle\leq C|r_{1}-r_{2}|^{\alpha}\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}+C\|b\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\int_{0}^{z}\left|\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}(r_{1},s)-\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}(r_{2},s)\right|ds.

Here, we have used the fact that
b\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq M_{0}<1
which implies
A(b(\Phi))\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3},M_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
, the fact that
\Psi_{s}\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)
and that
w\in C^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Therefore, invoking Grönwalls inequality yields

\left|\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}(r_{1},z)-\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}(r_{2},z)\right|\leq C\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}|r_{1}-r_{2}|^{\alpha}.

The same computations can be carried out to show that

\left|\frac{\partial w}{\partial y}(r_{1},z)-\frac{\partial w}{\partial y}(r_{2},z)\right|\leq C\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}|r_{1}-r_{2}|^{\alpha}.

and conclude bound (
2.30
). It should be stressed that the constants we obtain depend on
\alpha
, and on
L
and
\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}
via the estimates we get from Grönwall’s inequality. However, this
C
is an increasing function of
\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}
, which is bounded from above by
M_{0}
. Thus, we can get a
C=C(\alpha,L)
as claimed in the statement.

To show the difference estimate (
2.31
), we have to repeat the previous ideas. For the rest of the proof, we will use the notation
\widetilde{(\cdot)}=(\cdot)^{1}-(\cdot)^{2}
to denote the difference of two quantities. For
i=1,2
, denote by
w_{i}
the solution of (
2.35
) associated to the magnetic field
b^{i}
with initial condition
j_{0}^{i}
. Analogously, denote by
\Phi^{i}
to the change of variables arising from the characteristic equation with magnetic field
b^{i}
. Then, by using the explicit formula
j^{i}=w^{i}\circ(\Phi^{i})^{-1}
, we have that

(2.37)

\widetilde{j}=\widetilde{w}\circ(\Phi^{1})^{-1}+w^{2}\circ(\Phi^{1})^{-1}-w^{2}\circ(\Phi^{2})^{-1}.

The last two terms on the right hand side in (
2.37
) are bounded by

\left|w^{2}\circ(\Phi^{1})^{-1}(x,y,z)-w^{2}\circ(\Phi^{2})^{-1}(x,y,z)\right|\leq\|w\|_{C^{1}}\left|\widetilde{\Psi_{z}}\right|\leq\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\left|\widetilde{\Psi_{z}}\right|.

Moreover,
\widetilde{\Psi_{z}}
satisfies the ODE

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\partial\widetilde{X}}{\partial z}=\frac{\widetilde{b_{x}}b_{3}^{2}-b_{1}^{2}\widetilde{b_{3}}}{(1+b^{1}_{z})(1+b_{3}^{2})},\\
\\
\frac{\partial\widetilde{Y}}{\partial z}=\frac{\widetilde{b_{y}}b_{3}^{2}-b_{2}^{2}\widetilde{b_{3}}}{(1+b^{1}_{z})(1+b_{3}^{2})},\end{array}\right.

with initial data
\widetilde{X}(x,y,0)=0,\widetilde{Y}(x,y,0)=0
. Integrating between
0
and
z
, we readily see that

\left|\widetilde{\Psi_{z}}(x,y)\right|\leq\frac{2M_{0}}{1-M_{0}}\|\widetilde{b}\|_{\infty}.

To bound the first on the right hand side in (
2.37
) we first notice that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\partial\widetilde{w}}{\partial z}=A_{b^{2}}(\Phi^{2})\cdot\widetilde{w}+w^{1}\left(A_{b^{1}}(\Phi^{1})-A_{b^{2}}(\Phi^{2})\right)&z>0,\\
\widetilde{w}=\widetilde{j_{0}}&z=0.\end{array}\right.

Next, since

\|A(b^{1}(\Phi^{1}))-A(b^{2}(\Phi^{2}))\|_{\infty}\leq\|A(b^{1}(\Phi^{1}))\|_{\infty}\|\widetilde{\Phi}\|_{\infty}+\|A\widetilde{b}\|_{\infty}\leq C\|\widetilde{b}\|_{\infty},

we conclude after integrating in
z
that

\left|\widetilde{w}\right|\leq C\left(\|\widetilde{j_{0}}\|_{\infty}+\|j_{0}\|_{\infty}\|\widetilde{b}\|_{\infty}\right)\int_{0}^{z}|\widetilde{w}|ds.

An application of Grönwalls inequality yields the desired estimate. To show the complete
C^{1,\alpha}
norm estimate one can argue analogously.
∎

For further reference, we include here the following result, which is a corollary of Proposition
2.15
. This will be used later in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Corollary 2.17
.

Consider
M_{0}<1
,
b\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
j_{0}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{3})
as in Proposition
2.15
. Let
j\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
be the unique solution of (
2.29
), and
\bar{\jmath}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
be the unique solution of

(2.38)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{3}\bar{\jmath}+\left(b\cdot\nabla\right)\bar{\jmath}=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\bar{\jmath}=j_{0}&\text{on }\partial\Omega_{-}.\end{array}\right.

Then, there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,L)
such that
\delta j=j-\bar{\jmath}
satifies

(2.39)

\|\delta j\,\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}.

Moreover, if we have two magnetic fields
b^{1}
and
b^{2}
, and two initial conditions
j_{0}^{1}
, and
j_{0}^{2}
, we can estimate their difference:

(2.40)

\|\delta j^{1}-\delta j^{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\leq C\|b^{1}-b^{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\left(\|j_{0}^{1}-j_{0}^{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}}+\|\delta j^{1}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\|b^{1}-b^{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\right).

Proof.

We know that the solution for (
2.38
) is given by
\bar{\jmath}=j_{0}\circ\Phi^{-1}
, where
\Phi
is given by (
2.33
). We need to estimate the difference
\delta j=(w-j_{0})\circ\Phi^{-1}
where
w-j_{0}
satisfies the following problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\partial(w-j_{0})}{\partial z}=A_{b}\cdot w=A_{b}\cdot(w-j_{0})+A_{b}\cdot j_{0}&z>0,\\
w-j_{0}=0&z=0.\end{array}\right.

The rest of the proof follows after applying the same ideas as in the Proposition above, with the main difference that now the initial condition is zero. This allows us to obtain estimates that depend directly on
\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}
. For example, for the
L^{\infty}
norm of
w-j_{0}
we can integrate the ODE above, and after using Grönwall’s inequality,

\|w-j_{0}\|_{\infty}\leq C\|b\|_{C^{1}}\|j_{0}\|_{\infty}.

The
C^{1,\alpha}
bounds to show (
2.39
) follow similarly.
In order to show estimate (
2.39
) for the difference, one can mimic the same ideas done in Proposition
2.15
to show (
2.31
).
∎

2.4.3.
Transport divergence condition along characteristics

To conclude this subsection, we provide an important result regarding the transport nature of the divergence free condition along characteristics. A similar result was already provided in Alber
[
2
]
. However, in
[
2
]
the magnetic field
B
and hence the current
j
(being more precise, the fluid velocity field
v
and the vorticity
\omega
) is one derivative more regular which allows to understand the operators and the equation in the pointwise sense. We need to modify and adapt the argument to handle our regularity hypothesis.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
b\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)
be a divergence free vector field that satisfies the hypothesis in Proposition
2.15
and
B=(0,0,1)+b
. Assume that
j_{0}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)
and denote the unique solution to (
2.29
) by
j
. Assume further that
\text{div}\,j|_{\partial\Omega_{-}}=0
. Then,
\text{div}\,j=0
in
\Omega
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that

(2.41)

B\cdot\nabla\text{div}j=0,\ \mbox{ in }\Omega,

is satisfied in the sense of distributions. More precisely, we will show that

(2.42)

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}\left(\text{div}j\right)B\cdot\nabla\varphi=\left.\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\left(\text{div}j\right)\,B_{3}\,\varphi\right|^{z=L}_{z=0},\ \forall\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]).

Testing the left hand side of (
2.29
) against
\nabla\varphi
and integrating by parts we find that

\begin{split}\sum_{\ell,j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}B_{\ell}\,\partial_{\ell}j_{j}\,\partial_{j}\varphi&=\sum_{\ell,j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}\partial_{\ell}\left(B_{\ell}\,j_{j}\,\partial_{j}\varphi\right)-\sum_{\ell,j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}B_{\ell}\,j_{j}\partial_{\ell}\,\partial_{j}\varphi\\
&=\left.\left\langle B_{3}\,j\cdot\nabla\varphi\right\rangle\right|^{z=L}_{z=0}-\sum_{\ell,j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}B_{\ell}\,j_{j}\,\partial_{\ell}\partial_{j}\varphi,\end{split}

using that
B
is divergence-free. The same argument applied to the right hand side of (
2.29
) yields

\begin{split}\sum_{\ell,j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}j_{\ell}\,\partial_{\ell}B_{j}\,\partial_{j}\varphi&=\left.\left\langle j_{3}\,B\cdot\nabla\varphi\right\rangle\right|^{z=L}_{z=0}-\sum_{\ell,j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}\left(\partial_{\ell}j_{\ell}\right)B_{j}\,\partial_{j}\varphi\\
&-\sum_{\ell,j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}j_{\ell}\,B_{j}\,\partial_{\ell}\partial_{j}\varphi.\end{split}

Combining both identities we obtain that

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}\left(\text{div}\,j\right)\,B\cdot\nabla\varphi=\left.\left\langle j_{3}\,B\cdot\nabla\varphi\right\rangle\right|^{z=L}_{z=0}-\left.\left\langle B_{3}\,j\cdot\nabla\varphi\right\rangle\right|^{z=L}_{z=0}.

The terms on the right hand side containing
\partial_{3}\varphi
cancel out. Therefore, integrating by parts in
x,y
we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\langle j_{3}\,B\cdot\nabla\varphi\right\rangle-\left\langle B_{3}\,j\cdot\nabla\varphi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle j_{3}\,B_{1}\,\partial_{1}\varphi\right\rangle+\left\langle j_{3}\,B_{2}\,\partial_{2}\varphi\right\rangle-\left\langle B_{3}\,j_{1}\,\partial_{1}\varphi\right\rangle-\left\langle B_{3}\,j_{2}\,\partial_{2}\varphi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188pt=-\left\langle\partial_{1}j_{3}\,B_{1}\,\varphi\right\rangle-\left\langle\partial_{2}j_{3}\,B_{2}\,\varphi\right\rangle+\left\langle j_{3}\,\partial_{3}B_{3}\,\varphi\right\rangle+\left\langle\partial_{1}B_{3}\,j_{1}\,\varphi\right\rangle+\left\langle\partial_{2}B_{3}\,j_{2}\,\varphi\right\rangle-\left\langle B_{3}\,\partial_{3}j_{3}\,\varphi\right\rangle+\left\langle B_{3}\,\text{div}j\,\varphi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(j\cdot\nabla)B\rangle-\langle(B\cdot\nabla)j\rangle+\left\langle B_{3}\,\text{div}j\,\varphi\right\rangle=\left\langle B_{3}\,\text{div}j\,\varphi\right\rangle,

yielding (
2.42
). The result follows after we show the uniqueness of weak solutions. To that purpose, we will use the following duality argument. Take
\psi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L])
an arbitrary function and consider the following
backwards
transport problem

(2.43)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}B\cdot\nabla\varphi=\psi,&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\varphi=0,&\text{on }\partial\Omega_{+}.\end{array}\right.

Recalling that
B=(0,0,1)+(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})
and
\|b\|_{C^{2}}<1
by hypothesis, we can use the method of characteristics to solve equation (
2.43
). The resulting characteristic curves yield a
C^{2}
change of coordinates, and given that
\psi\in C^{2}
we have that
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L])
. To conclude, using the fact that
\varphi=0
on
\partial\Omega_{+}
, and by hypothesis
\text{div}j=0
on
\partial\Omega_{-}
, we find that both terms in the right hand side of (
2.42
) lead to no contribution. Thus,

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]}\text{div}j\cdot\psi=0,\forall\psi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]),

showing that
\text{div}j=0
in
\Omega
.
∎

3.
The linearized problem

The main purpose of this section is to describe the formal idea behind the method illustrated in the introduction to construct a solution
(B,p)
of the magnetohydrostatic equations satisfying the boundary value conditions (
1.9
). Although we avoid any technicalities and remark that the exposed ideas are purely formal, we believe that the following digression is instructive to understand the core ideas towards the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

To that purpose, we have to define an operator
\Gamma
which has a fixed point
b
such that
B=(0,0,1)+b
is a solution to (
1.9
). Following the ideas developed by Alber in
[
2
]
(as well as in subsequent works
[
3
,
4
]
) we divide the problem into two building blocks: a first order transport-type equation for the current
j
, and a div-curl problem for the magnetic field
B
. Given,
b\in C^{2,\alpha}
, we define
j\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)
as the solution to the transport equation

(3.1)

\begin{cases}((0,0,1)+b)\cdot\nabla)j=(j\cdot\nabla)((0,0,1)+b)\quad\mbox{in }\Omega,\\
j=j_{0}\quad\mbox{on }\partial\Omega_{-}\end{cases}

Expanding the equation, we readily check that

\partial_{3}j+\frac{b_{1}}{1+b_{3}}\partial_{1}j+\frac{b_{2}}{1+b_{3}}\partial_{2}j=A(b)j,

where the matrix
A(b)
reads

(3.2)

A(b)=(a_{\ell j})_{\ell,j},\text{ with }a_{\ell j}=\frac{\partial_{j}b_{\ell}}{1+b_{3}},\quad\mbox{ for }\ell,j=1,2,3.

If
b
is a small perturbation,
\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)}\ll 1
, and dropping the small nonlinear terms, the resulting equation simply reads
\partial_{3}j=0
in
\Omega
and hence,
j
remains constant along the vertical variable, this is

(3.3)

j(x,y,z)=j(x,y)=j_{0}(x,y)\mbox{ in }\Omega.

Notice that
j_{0}(x,y)
is an unknown of the problem.
Taking into account this approximation, we solve the div-curl problem to recover a new magnetic field
B
. Since
B=(0,0,1)+b
, then the resulting problem is given by

(3.4)

\begin{cases}\nabla\times B=j_{0}(x,y)\quad\mbox{in }\Omega,\\
\nabla\cdot B=0\quad\mbox{in }\Omega,\\
B\cdot n=f\quad\mbox{on }\partial\Omega,\end{cases}

and complemented with the integral conditions

\int_{\{x=0\}}B\cdot d\vec{S}=J_{1}\quad\text{ and }\quad\int_{\{y=0\}}B\cdot d\vec{S}=J_{2}.

The fluxes along the two directions of the period of the torus are a degree of freedom of the div-curl problem (
3.4
). Those conditions will be used in the sequel to obtain a uni-valued pressure function. Later, we will also impose that the constructed solution
B=(0,0,1)+b
also satisfies the boundary condition

(3.5)

B_{\tau}=g=(g_{1},g_{2})\quad\mbox{on }\partial\Omega_{-}.

In order to solve (
3.4
), we use a vector potential
Z
. More precisely, we transform (
3.4
) into an elliptic problem for
Z
. To that purpose, we define

(3.6)

B=\nabla\times Z+A(0,0,1),\quad\mbox{ where }A=\int_{\partial\Omega_{-}}f=\int_{\partial\Omega_{+}}f.

The newly defined potential vector
Z
solves the following auxiliary problem

(3.7)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta Z=j_{0}&\text{in }\Omega,\\
Z_{2}=h_{2}^{-},\,Z_{1}=h_{1}^{-}&\text{on }\partial\Omega_{-},\\
Z_{2}=h_{2}^{+}-\frac{J_{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}},\,Z_{1}=h_{1}^{+}+\frac{J_{1}}{(2\pi)^{2}}&\text{on }\partial\Omega_{+},\\
\partial_{3}Z=-\partial_{1}Z_{1}-\partial_{2}Z_{2}&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

where the different
h^{\pm}_{\ell}
(
\ell=1,2
) are defined as

(3.8)

\begin{split}h_{2}^{-}(x,y)&=\int_{0}^{x}f(s,y,0)\,ds-\frac{x}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(s,y,0)\,ds,\\
h_{1}^{-}(x,y)&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{y}f(s,t,0)\,dtds+\frac{y}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(s,t,0)\,dtds,\\
h_{2}^{+}(x,y)&=\int_{0}^{x}f(s,y,L)\,ds-\frac{x}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(s,y,L)\,ds,\\
h_{1}^{+}(x,y)&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{y}f(s,t,L)\,dtds+\frac{y}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(s,t,L)\,dtds.\end{split}

System (
3.7
) consists on two uncoupled Dirichlet problems for
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
, and a Neumann problem for
Z_{3}
. The first two problems for
Z_{1},Z_{2}
can be solved without any further complication, but the Neumann problem for
Z_{3}
requires that
j
satisfies an integrability condition, which is indeed a consequence of
j_{0}^{3}
having zero mean and imposing that
j
is divergence free. Furthermore, one obtains as an easy consequence that
Z
has zero divergence. Indeed, the divergence free condition on
j
and the choice of
Z_{3}
implies that
\text{div}\,Z
is harmonic with zero boundary conditions, i.e.
\text{div}\,Z
vanishes. Therefore, formula (
3.6
) gives us a solution for the div-curl system.
Similarly as in the two dimensional case
[
4
, Section 2]
, in order to solve the Dirichlet elliptic problem for
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
, one can apply Fourier transform in the two periodic variables
x,y
. This transforms the two elliptic problems into two uncoupled second-order non-homogeneous ODE with constant coefficients. After using variation of parameters method we find that

\displaystyle Z_{1}(r,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{\sinh(|\xi|L)-\sinh(|\xi|z)-\sinh(|\xi|(L-z))}{|\xi|^{2}\sinh(|\xi|L)}\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-2.34525pt\hbox{$j$}\hskip-2.34525pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.59537pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}_{0}^{1}(\xi)e^{ir\cdot\xi}

(3.9)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt}_{2}^{+}(\xi)\frac{\sinh(|\xi|z)}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}e^{ir\cdot\xi}-\frac{z}{(2\pi)L},J_{2}.

where we used the notation
r=(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
. In the previous sums and throughout the rest of the article, the zero Fourier mode
\xi=0
is understood as the limit as
\xi\rightarrow 0
. More precisely, if
\xi=0
we will use that

\frac{\sinh(|\xi|(L-z))}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}\rightarrow\frac{L-z}{L},\qquad\frac{\sinh(|\xi|z)}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}\rightarrow\frac{z}{L},

and

\frac{\sinh(|\xi|L)-\sinh(|\xi|z)-\sinh(|\xi|(L-z))}{|\xi|^{2}\sinh(|\xi|L)}\rightarrow\frac{Lz}{2}\left(1-\frac{z}{L}\right).

Furthermore, for further reference, if
\xi=0
we follow the convention

(3.11)

\frac{\cosh(|\xi|L)-1}{|\xi|\sinh(|\xi|L)}\rightarrow\frac{L}{2},\quad\frac{|\xi|}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}\rightarrow\frac{1}{L},\quad\frac{|\xi|\cosh(|\xi|L)}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}\rightarrow\frac{1}{L}.

In order to compute
Z_{3}
, one can solve the Neumann problem, but it can be easily deduced
a posteriori
by means of the divergence free condition. Indeed, assuming that the constructed solution is divergence free we obtain

(3.12)

\partial_{1}Z_{1}+\partial_{2}Z_{2}+\partial_{3}Z_{3}=0,

we have that
\partial_{3}^{2}Z_{3}=-\partial_{3}\partial_{1}Z_{1}-\partial_{3}\partial_{2}Z_{2}.

Taking the Fourier transform in the first two variables, the equation reads

(3.13)

Here
f(z)
denotes an arbitrary function and each node of the lattice
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is written as
\xi=(m,n)
. Moreover, we denote
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{*}=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
. Using the zero Fourier mode of the divergence free condition (
3.12
) we find that
f^{\prime}(z)=0
. Thus,
f(z)
is constant and since
B
is defined as (
3.6
) we can assume without loss of generality that
f(z)=0
.

Hence, we have computed the potential vector field
Z=(Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3})
given by the formulas (
3.9
)-(
3
) solving (
3.7
). Hence, recalling (
3.6
), we can recover
B
that satisfies (
3.4
). The main issue of this construction is that
j_{0}(x,y)
is still an unknown of the problem. On the other hand, the constructed solution
B
does not satisfies the tangential boundary condition (
3.5
). The key idea to circumvent this problem is to choose
j_{0}(x,y)
in (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and (
3
) in such a way
that (
3.5
) is satisfied. This will yield an integral equation for the unknown current
j_{0}(x,y)
. More precisely, since
B_{\tau}=g
on
\partial\Omega_{-}
, we have that

\displaystyle\partial_{2}Z_{3}(r,0)-\partial_{3}Z_{2}(r,0)=g_{1}(r)

\displaystyle\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{-},

\displaystyle\partial_{3}Z_{1}(r,0)-\partial_{1}Z_{3}(r,0)=g_{2}(r)

\displaystyle\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{-}.

Therefore, imposing the first condition on the vector potential
Z
and using the explicit formulas for
Z_{2}
and
Z_{3}
given in (
3.10
), (
3
) respectively, we obtain

On the other hand if
\xi\neq 0
the system (
3.22
)-(
3.23
) has three unknowns
j_{0}=(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2},j_{0}^{3})
. Furthermore, recall that we have a constraint on the divergence of the current
j
, i.e. ,
\nabla\cdot j=0
. This allows to reduce the number of independent functions from three to two. More precisely, in the particular linearized setting under consideration, (
3.3
) implies that
\partial_{3}j_{0}^{3}=0
. Therefore the divergence free condition for
j
yields

Then, for
\xi\neq 0
, the system of equations (
3.22
)-(
3.25
) can be inverted since

(3.26)

\mbox{det}A=-(\mathfrak{m}(\xi))^{-1}\neq 0,\mbox{ where }A=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{mn}{|\xi|^{2}}&\frac{n^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}-1&\frac{in}{|\xi|^{2}}(\mathfrak{m}(\xi))^{-1}\\
-\frac{m^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}+1&-\frac{mn}{|\xi|^{2}}&-\frac{im}{|\xi|^{2}}(\mathfrak{m}(\xi))^{-1}\\
im&in&0\end{array}\right).

In addition, we have that

(3.27)

A^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{mn}{|\xi|^{2}}&\frac{n^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}&\frac{-im}{|\xi|^{2}}\\
-\frac{m^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}&-\frac{mn}{|\xi|^{2}}&-\frac{in}{|\xi|^{2}}\\
-in\mathfrak{m}(\xi)&im\mathfrak{m}(\xi)&0\end{array}\right),

and therefore, we can invert the linear system and obtain that for
\xi\neq 0

Hence, expression for
j_{0}
in (
3.29
) yields the value of
Z
by means of formulas (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and (
3
) and thus the value of a solution
B
via (
3.6
) solving the desired boundary value problem (
3.4
)-(
3.5
). However, the values
J_{1},J_{2}
appearing in (
3.29
) are until now undetermined. We will fix both values in order to guarantee that the pressure
p
(which is also an unknown of the problem) is a uni-valued function in
\Omega
. Indeed using the first equation in (
1.9
) and linearizing around
B=(0,0,1)+b
we find that

(3.30)

j^{2}(x,y,z)=\partial_{x}p(x,y,z),\quad-j^{1}(x,y,z)=\partial_{y}p(x,y,z),\quad 0=\partial_{z}p(x,y,z).

Equation (
3.30
) shows that
p(x,y,z)=p(x,y)
and therefore
j^{1}(x,y,z)=j^{1}_{0}(x,y)
and
j^{2}(x,y,z)=j^{2}_{0}(x,y)
as well. Moreover, (
3.30
) implies that
\nabla p=\nabla p_{|\partial\Omega_{-}}=(j^{1}_{0},j^{2}_{0})
. Hence, we can obtain a solution to (
3.30
)

p(x,y,z)=\int_{0}^{\textbf{{x}}}j_{0}(x,y)\ d\textbf{{x}},

where the integral on the right hand side in the line integration computed along any contour that connects
(0,0,0)
and
\textbf{{x}}\in\Omega
. Therefore, to ensure that the pressure
p(x,y,z)
is a uni-valued function in
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]
we need that

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}j^{1}(x,y,0)\ dxdy=0,\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}j^{2}(x,y,0)dxdy=0.

Using (
3.29
) we obtain that

\frac{J_{1}}{\pi L^{2}}=\langle\textsf{G}_{2}\rangle,\quad\frac{J_{2}}{\pi L^{2}}=\langle\textsf{G}_{1}\rangle.

4.
The non-linear setting: the integral equation for the current

In this section, we will follow the same arguments used in Section
3
but taking into account the whole non-linear problem. In particular, we will derive an integral equation for the current
j
on
\partial\Omega_{-}
, namely,
j_{0}(x,y)
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
. As expected, the integral equation will be a perturbation of equation (
3.29
) in terms of the perturbed magnetic field
b
and the boundary values
f
and
g
. During this section, the computations will be again formal and we will not consider convergence issues that might arise from the Fourier series. The following section (cf. Section
5
) is devoted to justify the convergence of the Fourier series and the precise definitions of the operators derived hereafter.

Given,
b\in C^{2,\alpha}
and
B=(0,0,1)+b
we define
j\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)
as the solution to

(4.1)

\begin{cases}((0,0,1)+b)\cdot\nabla)j=(j\cdot\nabla)((0,0,1)+b)\quad\mbox{in }\Omega,\\
j=j_{0}\quad\mbox{on }\partial\Omega_{-}.\end{cases}

More precisely, we can write the previous system as

(4.2)

\partial_{3}j+\frac{b_{1}}{1+b_{3}}\partial_{1}j+\frac{b_{2}}{1+b_{3}}\partial_{2}j=A(b)j,

where the matrix
A(b)
reads

(4.3)

A(b)=(a_{\ell j})_{\ell,j},\text{ with }a_{\ell j}=\frac{\partial_{j}b_{\ell}}{1+b_{3}}.

The system can be solved by means of characteristics. Indeed, using Proposition
2.15
,
the solution is explicitly given by

(4.4)

j(x,y,z)=w\circ\Phi^{-1}(x,y,z),

where
w
satisfies the differential equation (
2.35
) and
\Phi
is the flow mapping defined by the characteristic lines of (
4.1
) given in (
2.32
)-(
2.33
). Notice that a priori, the value of the
j_{0}\mbox{ on }\partial\Omega_{-}
is unknown.

As in the linearizing setting (cf. equations (
3.4
)-(
3.5
) in Section
3
), we next solve the div-curl problem to obtain a new field
W
, namely,

(4.5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\nabla\times W=j&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div}\,W=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
W\cdot n=f&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\\
W_{\tau}=g&\text{on }\partial\Omega_{-},\end{array}\right.

complemented with the integral conditions

\int_{\{x=0\}}W\cdot d\vec{S}=\frac{J_{1}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\int_{\{y=0\}}W\cdot d\vec{S}=\frac{J_{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}}.

In order to solve (
4.5
), we use a divergence free vector potential
Z
, namely

W=\nabla\times Z+A(0,0,1),\quad\mbox{ where }A=\int_{\partial\Omega_{-}}f=\int_{\partial\Omega_{+}}f

The resulting problem (identical as in (
3.7
) in Section
3
) reads

(4.6)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta Z=j&\text{in }\Omega,\\
Z_{2}=h_{2}^{-},\,Z_{1}=h_{1}^{-}&\text{on }\partial\Omega_{-},\\
Z_{2}=h_{2}^{+}-\frac{J_{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}},\,Z_{1}=h_{1}^{+}+\frac{J_{1}}{(2\pi)^{2}}&\text{on }\partial\Omega_{+},\\
\partial_{3}Z=-\partial_{1}Z_{1}-\partial_{2}Z_{2},&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

where the different
h^{\pm}_{i}
(
i=1,2
) are defined as in (
3.8
) and
j=j(x,y,z)
is given by (
4.4
). This leads again to an uncoupled problem for the first two components
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
. Following the approach in
[
4
, Section 3.1]
, by means of the Green’s function for the Laplace operator, the unique solutions
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are

\displaystyle Z_{1}(r,z)

(4.7)

\mathfrak{G}(\xi,z;z_{0})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\sinh(|\xi|z)\sinh(|\xi|(L-z_{0}))}{|\xi|\sinh(|\xi|L)}&z<z_{0},\\
\\
\ \frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\sinh(|\xi|z_{0})\sinh(|\xi|(L-z))}{|\xi|\sinh(|\xi|L)}&z\geq z_{0},\end{array}\right.

is the Green function solving the problem

(4.8)

\begin{cases}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,z;z_{0})+|\xi|^{2}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,z;z_{0})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\delta(z-z_{0}),\ \mbox{ in }\Omega,\\
\mathfrak{G}=0,\ \mbox{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

451.29065pt\hskip-2.88078pt\hbox{$h$}\hskip-2.88078pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}_{2}^{+}(\xi)\frac{in}{|\xi|}\frac{\cosh(|\xi|z)}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}e^{ir\cdot\xi}.

Once we have found the only possible solutions for the vector potential
Z
, we can use the remaining tangential boundary condition
W_{\tau}=g
on
\partial\Omega_{-}
which in terms of potential
Z
reads

\displaystyle\partial_{2}Z_{3}(r,0)-\partial_{3}Z_{2}(r,0)=g_{1}(r)

\displaystyle\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{-},

\displaystyle\partial_{3}Z_{1}(r,0)-\partial_{1}Z_{3}(r,0)=g_{2}(r)

\displaystyle\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{-},

to obtain an integral equation for the current
j
. Notice that the resulting formulas are very similar to those obtained in the linearized case with the only difference that the current expression for
j
is more involved. After some lengthy but straightforward computations we find that

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{1}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{*}}\frac{in}{|\xi|^{2}}j^{3}(\zeta,0)e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}\,d\zeta+\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\frac{mn}{|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{\{\xi\neq 0\}}\right)\partial_{3}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,0,z_{0})\,j^{1}(\zeta,z_{0})\,e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}d\zeta dz_{0}

(4.10)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\frac{n^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{\{\xi\neq 0\}}-1\right)\partial_{3}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,0,z_{0})\,j^{2}(\zeta,z_{0})\,e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}d\zeta dz_{0}+\frac{J_{2}}{(2\pi)L},

where
\widetilde{g}_{1}(r)=g_{1}(r)-\mathcal{Z}_{1}(r)
and
\mathcal{Z}_{1}(r)
depends only on the boundary value
f
and is given in (
3.16
). Analogously,

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{2}(r)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{*}}\frac{im}{|\xi|^{2}}j^{3}(\zeta,0)e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}\,d\zeta-\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{\{\xi\neq 0\}}-1\right)\partial_{3}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,0,z_{0})\,j^{1}(\zeta,z_{0})\,e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}d\zeta dz_{0}

(4.11)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\frac{mn}{|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{\{\xi\neq 0\}}\right)\partial_{3}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,0,z_{0})\,j^{2}(\zeta,z_{0})\,e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}d\zeta dz_{0}+\frac{J_{1}}{(2\pi)L}.

Here,
\widetilde{g}_{2}(r)=g_{2}(r)-\mathcal{Z}_{2}(r)
and
\mathcal{Z}_{2}(r)
is defined in (
3.18
). In order to write a more convenient expression for the current, let us introduce the operator

(4.12)

\mathcal{A}[\theta](r,z)=\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\partial_{3}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,0,z_{0})\theta(\zeta,z_{0})e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}\,d\zeta dz_{0}.

In particular, for
\theta(r,z)=j^{\ell}_{0}\circ\Phi^{-1}
,
\ell\in\{1,2\}
we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[j^{\ell}_{0}\circ\Phi^{-1}](r,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\partial_{3}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,0,z_{0})\left(j^{\ell}_{0}\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)(\zeta,z_{0})e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}\,d\zeta dz_{0}

(4.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{\sinh(|\xi|(L-z_{0}))}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}\left(j^{\ell}_{0}\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)(\zeta,z_{0})e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}\,d\zeta dz_{0},

where the normal derivative of
\mathfrak{G}(\xi,z;z_{0})
at
z=0
, i.e.
\partial_{3}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,0,z_{0})
has been computed using (
4.7
). Furthermore, we make the change of variable given by diffeomorphism
g(\eta)=(X(\eta,s),Y(\eta,s))
with
X,Y
satisfying (
2.32
) and with inverse
(X^{-1},Y^{-1})
. The Jacobian is defined by

J_{g}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{1}X&\partial_{2}X\\
\partial_{1}Y&\partial_{2}Y\end{array}\right)(\eta,s)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{1}X^{-1}&\partial_{2}X^{-1}\\
\partial_{1}Y^{-1}&\partial_{2}Y^{-1}\end{array}\right)^{-1}\left(X(\eta,s),Y(\eta,s)\right).

Denoting by
\Theta(\eta,s)=|J_{g}|-1
, (
4.13
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[j^{\ell}_{0}\circ\Phi^{-1}](r,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{\sinh(|\xi|(L-s))}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}\frac{j^{\,\ell}_{0}\left(\eta\right)}{1+\Theta(\eta,s)}e^{i\left(r-\Phi(\eta,s)\right)\cdot\xi}\,d\eta ds

(4.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathcal{G}(r-\eta,\eta)j_{0}^{\,\ell}(\eta)d\eta,

with

(4.15)

\mathcal{G}(r,\eta)=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}a_{\xi}(\eta)e^{ir\cdot\xi},\ \mbox{where }a_{\xi}(\eta)=\int_{0}^{L}\frac{\sinh(|\xi|(L-s))}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}\frac{e^{i\Lambda(\eta,s)\cdot\xi}}{1+\Theta(\eta,s)}ds.

Furthermore, using the same decomposition as in
[
4
, Section 3.2]
, we can write the kernel (
4.15
) as

\mathcal{G}(r,\eta)=\mathcal{G}_{0}(r,\eta)+\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\mathcal{G}_{\kappa}(r,\eta),

where

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{0}(r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathfrak{m}(\xi)e^{ir\cdot\xi},

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}(r,\eta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}e^{ir\cdot\xi}\int_{0}^{L}e^{-|\xi|s}\left(\frac{e^{-i\xi\cdot\Lambda(\eta,s)}-1}{1+\Theta(\eta,s)}\right)ds,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}(r,\eta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}e^{ir\cdot\xi}\int_{0}^{L}e^{-|\xi|s}\left(\frac{\Theta(\eta,s)}{1+\Theta(\eta,s)}\right)ds,

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{3}(r,\eta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}e^{ir\cdot\xi}\int_{0}^{L}M(\xi,s)\left(\frac{e^{-i\xi\cdot\Lambda(\eta,s)}-1}{1+\Theta(\eta,s)}\right)ds,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{4}(r,\eta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}e^{ir\cdot\xi}\int_{0}^{L}M(\xi,s)\left(\frac{\Theta(\eta,s)}{1+\Theta(\eta,s)}\right)ds.

We recall that the multiplier
\mathfrak{m}(\xi)
is defined in (
3.19
) and the function
M(\xi,s)
is defined as

(4.21)

M(\xi,s)=\frac{e^{-2|\xi|L}\left(e^{|\xi|s}-e^{-|\xi|s}\right)}{1-e^{-2|\xi|L}}.

Using decomposition (
4.16
)-(
4.20
), we rewrite the operator
\mathcal{A}[j^{\ell}_{0}\circ\Phi^{-1}]
in (
4.14
) as

(4.22)

\mathcal{A}[j^{\ell}_{0}\circ\Phi^{-1}](r,z)=\mathcal{T}j^{\ell}_{0}=\mathcal{T}_{0}j^{\ell}_{0}+\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}j^{\ell}_{0}

where

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{0}j^{\ell}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathcal{G}_{0}(r-\eta)j_{0}^{\ell}(\eta)d\eta,\mbox{ for }\ell=1,2,

(4.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\kappa}j^{\ell}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathcal{G}_{\kappa}(r-\eta,\eta)j_{0}^{\ell}(\eta)d\eta,\mbox{ for }\kappa=1,\ldots,4,\ \ell=1,2.

It is important to notice the operator
\mathcal{T}_{0}
coincides with the operator
\mathsf{T}
in Section
3
given by means of the multiplier function
\mathfrak{m}(\xi)=\frac{\cosh(|\xi|L)-1}{|\xi|\sinh(|\xi|L)}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is an operator that can be inverted as long as
(\mathfrak{m}(\xi))^{-1}\neq 0
, fact that we have already shown in (
3.26
) in Section
3
.

We may use now the previous digression to help us deriving the integral equation for
j_{0}
. To that purpose, we denote by
\delta j=j-\bar{\jmath}
(notation introduced in Corollary
2.17
) to write

(4.25)

j(x,y,z)=\delta j+\bar{\jmath}=[(w-j_{0})\circ\Phi^{-1}+j_{0}\circ\Phi^{-1}](x,y,z),\ \mbox{ in }\Omega,

where
w-j_{0}
satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\partial(w-j_{0})}{\partial z}=A_{b}\cdot(w-j_{0})+A_{b}\cdot j_{0}&z>0,\\
w-j_{0}=0,&z=0.\end{array}\right.

Hence, using (
4.25
) and the definition of the operator (
4.12
), the second and third term on the right hand side in (
4
) are given by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\frac{mn}{|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{\{\xi\neq 0\}}\right)\partial_{3}\Phi(\xi,0,z_{0})\,j^{1}(\zeta,z_{0})\,e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}d\zeta dz_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\left(\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{1})+\mathcal{A}(j^{1}_{0})\right),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\frac{n^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{\{\xi\neq 0\}}-1\right)\partial_{3}\mathfrak{G}(\xi,0,z_{0})\,j^{2}(\zeta,z_{0})\,e^{i(r-\zeta)\cdot\xi}d\zeta dz_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{y}-\text{id}\right)\left(\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{2})+\mathcal{A}(j^{2}_{0})\right).

We recall that
\mathcal{R}_{x}
and
\mathcal{R}_{y}
are the Riesz transforms. Thus, we infer using (
4.22
) that

(4.26)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{1}-\frac{J_{2}}{(2\pi)L}-\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\mathcal{T}j_{0}^{1}+\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{y}-\text{id}\right)\mathcal{T}j_{0}^{2}+\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{*}}\frac{in}{|\xi|^{2}}\hbox{\hskip with
\mathcal{S}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{1})+\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{y}-\text{id}\right)\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{2})
. Similarly, for (
4
) we find that

(4.27)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{2}-\frac{J_{1}}{(2\pi)L}+\mathcal{S}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{x}-\text{id}\right)\mathcal{T}j_{0}^{1}-\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\mathcal{T}j_{0}^{2}-\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{*}}\frac{im}{|\xi|^{2}}\hbox{\hskip with
\mathcal{S}_{2}=\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{x}-\text{id}\right)\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{1})+\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{2})
. Furthermore, invoking decomposition (

), inverting the operator
\mathcal{T}_{0}
and denoting by

(4.28)

\textsf{T}_{\kappa}=\mathcal{T}_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\kappa},\quad\textsf{G}_{\ell}=\mathcal{T}_{0}^{-1}\tilde{g}_{\ell},\quad\mathsf{S}_{\ell}=\mathcal{T}_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\ell},

for

,

we obtain that (
4.26
) and (

) read

\displaystyle\textsf{G}_{1}-\frac{J_{2}}{\pi L^{2}}+\mathsf{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{1}+\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{y}-\text{id}\right)\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}j_{0}^{1}+\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{y}-\text{id}\right)j_{0}^{2}

(4.29)

The resulting integral equations (
4
)-(
4
) resemble equations (
3.22
)-(
3.23
) in Section
3
. The main difference is that in this nonlinear setting we have an extra perturbation we will have to estimate and control later on. Furthermore, similarly as in Section
3
, we need to include the div-free condition to obtain a well-defined system of equations (three unknowns and three equations). Using Proposition
2.18
, and recalling that
B
is divergence free, it is enough to impose that
\nabla\cdot j=0
on
\partial\Omega_{-}
, i.e.
\nabla\cdot j_{0}=0
. Furthermore,
\nabla\cdot j_{0}=\partial_{1}j^{1}_{0}+\partial_{2}j^{2}_{0}+\partial_{3}j^{3}_{0}
, so we can use the transport equation (
4.2
) to write
\partial_{3}j^{3}_{0}
in terms of
B
and the tangential derivatives of
j_{0}
, resulting in

0=\text{div}\,j_{0}=\partial_{1}j^{1}_{0}+\partial_{2}j^{2}_{0}+A(b)_{3\ell}j^{\ell}_{0}-\frac{b_{1}}{1+b_{3}}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}-\frac{b_{2}}{1+b_{3}}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0},

and hence

(4.31)

(1+b_{3})\partial_{1}j^{1}_{0}+(1+b_{3})\partial_{2}j^{2}_{0}+(1+b_{3})A(b)_{3\ell}j^{\ell}_{0}-b_{1}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}-b_{2}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0}=0,\quad\ell=1,2,3,

where the matrix
A(b)
is given in (
4.3
). Here, notation
A(b)_{3\ell}j^{\ell}_{0}
means summation over repeated indices, namely

A(b)_{3\ell}j^{\ell}_{0}=A(b)_{31}j^{1}_{0}+A(b)_{32}j^{2}_{0}+A(b)_{33}j^{3}_{0}.

Recalling (
3.2
) we can simplify expression (
4.31
) to find that

(4.32)

(1+b_{3})\partial_{1}j^{1}_{0}+(1+b_{3})\partial_{2}j^{2}_{0}+\partial_{\ell}b_{3}j^{\ell}_{0}-b_{1}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}-b_{2}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0}=0,\quad\ell=1,2,3,

By means of the previous computations, we infer that

\displaystyle\textsf{G}_{1}-\frac{J_{2}}{\pi L^{2}}+\mathsf{S}_{1}-\mathsf{H}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{1}+\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{y}-\text{id}\right)\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{2}-j_{0}^{2}

(4.33)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{*}}\frac{in}{|\xi|^{2}}(\mathfrak{m}(\xi))^{-1}\hbox{\hskip and hence since by the definition of the functions

in (

) we find that

(4.38)

Combining (
4
)-(
