451.29065pt\hskip-2.88078pt\hbox{$h$}\hskip-2.88078pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{-}_{2}(0)\frac{\chi_{\{\xi=0\}}}{L}\\
&=i\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.02144pt\hbox{$\partial_{1}h^{-}_{2}$}\hskip-10.02144pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(\xi)\frac{m}{|\xi|}\frac{\cosh(|\xi|L)}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}\chi_{\{\xi\neq 0\}}-\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-2.88078pt\hbox{$h$}\hskip-2.88078pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{-}_{2}(0)\frac{\chi_{\{\xi=0\}}}{L}.\end{split}

As a consequence,
\mathcal{Z}_{13}
is given by the map

(5.78)

f\to\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-2.88078pt\hbox{$h$}\hskip-2.88078pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}_{2}^{-}(\xi)\left(\frac{n^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{\{\xi\neq 0\}}-1\right)\frac{|\xi|\cosh(|\xi|L)}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}e^{ir\cdot\xi},

and can be understood as the multiplier

\frac{im}{|\xi|}\frac{\cosh(|\xi|L)}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}\chi_{\{\xi\neq 0\}}-\frac{\chi_{\{\xi=0\}}}{L},

applied to the function
\partial_{1}h_{2}^{-}
. It is easy to check that the multiplier

\frac{im}{|\xi|}\frac{\cosh(|\xi|L)}{\sinh(|\xi|L)}=\frac{im}{|\xi|}+\mathsf{r}(\xi),

is a Mihklin-Hörmander multiplier in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of degree
0
plus a smoothing multiplier
\mathsf{r}(\xi)
, so the map (
5.78
) is bounded from
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\to C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. As a consequence, we obtain the bound

\|\mathcal{Z}_{13}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq C\|\partial_{1}h^{-}_{2}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq C\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}.

Combining the derived bounds for
\mathcal{Z}_{1\kappa}
for
\kappa=1,\ldots,4,
shows the desired estimate for
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
concluding the proof.
∎

Therefore, using the previous lemma we can easily show the following bounds for the functions
\textsf{G}_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
.

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
f\in C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega),g\in C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})
. Then,
\textsf{G}_{\ell}=\mathcal{T}_{0}^{-1}\left(g_{\ell}-\mathcal{Z}_{\ell}\right)\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for
\ell=1,2
. More precisely, we have that

(5.79)

\left\lVert\textsf{G}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C\left(\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}+\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\right),\quad\ell=1,2.

Proof.

The proof follows easily by combining estimate (
5.75
) and recalling that

\widehat{\mathcal{T}_{0}^{-1}f}(\xi)=\frac{|\xi|\sinh(|\xi|L)}{\cosh(|\xi|L)-1}\widehat{f}(\xi).

Indeed, since

\frac{|\xi|\sinh(|\xi|L)}{\cosh(|\xi|L)-1}=|\xi|+\mathsf{r}(\xi),

is a Mikhlin-Hörmander multiplier in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of degree
1
plus a smoothing multiplier
\mathsf{r}(\xi)
we conclude that

(5.80)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\textsf{G}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{0}^{-1}\left(g_{\ell}-\mathcal{Z}_{\ell}\right)\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C\left\lVert g_{\ell}-\mathcal{Z}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq C\left(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\right),\quad\ell=1,2.

∎

5.4.
Hölder estimates for the operator
\mathsf{H}_{\ell}

In this subsection, we will derive Hölder estimates for the operator
\mathsf{H}_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
given by

(5.81)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{y}\left(b_{1}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}+b_{2}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0}-\partial_{\ell}b_{3}j^{\ell}_{0}-b_{3}\partial_{1}j_{0}^{1}-b_{3}\partial_{2}j_{0}^{2}\right),

(5.82)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{x}\left(b_{1}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}+b_{2}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0}-\partial_{\ell}b_{3}j^{\ell}_{0}-b_{3}\partial_{1}j_{0}^{1}-b_{3}\partial_{2}j_{0}^{2}\right).

We recall that
\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{B}_{y}
are defined via the multipliers

\widehat{\mathcal{B}_{x}f}(\xi)=-i\frac{m}{|\xi|^{2}}\widehat{f}(\xi),\ \widehat{\mathcal{B}_{y}f}(\xi)=-i\frac{n}{|\xi|^{2}}\widehat{f}(\xi).

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
b\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)
and
j_{0}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})
. Then,
\textsf{H}_{\ell}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for
\ell=1,2
. More precisely, we have that

(5.83)

\left\lVert\textsf{H}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C\left(\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\left\lVert j_{0}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\right),\quad\ell=1,2.

Furthermore, we have that

(5.84)

\left\lVert\textsf{H}_{\ell}[b_{1}]-{\textsf{H}_{\ell}[b_{2}]}\right\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\leq C\left(\left\lVert b_{1}-b_{2}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\left\lVert j_{0}\right\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\right),\quad\ell=1,2.

Proof.

The proof follows easily by noticing that the multipliers associated to
\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{B}_{y}
are Mikhlin-Hörmander multipliers in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of degree
-1
. Indeed, using the previous assertion we readily check that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\textsf{H}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\left(b_{1}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}+b_{2}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0}-\partial_{\ell}b_{3}j^{\ell}_{0}-b_{3}\partial_{1}j_{0}^{1}-b_{3}\partial_{2}j_{0}^{2}\right)\right\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}

(5.85)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\left\lVert j_{0}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}},\quad\ell=1,2.

The difference estimate (
5.84
) follows directly by the linearity of the operator.
∎

5.5.
Hölder estimates for the operator
\mathsf{S}_{\ell}

In this subsection, we established the estimates for the operator
\mathsf{S}_{\ell}
, is defined as

\mathsf{S}_{\ell}=\mathcal{T}_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
where

(5.86)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{1})+\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{y}-\text{id}\right)\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{2}),

(5.87)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{x}-\text{id}\right)\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{1})+\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{2}).

Therefore, in order to give a precise meaning and derive the Hölder bounds for the operator
\mathsf{S}_{\ell}
, we first need to analyze the operator
\mathcal{A}
introduced in (
4.12
). Although the expression (
4.12
) seems complicated, the regularity of the operator can be understood in simpler way. For a generic function
f\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)
, the function
\mathcal{A}(f)
equals
\partial_{3}g(r)
where
g\in C^{3,\alpha}(\Omega)
is the unique solution to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta g=f,&\text{in }\Omega,\\
g=0,&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

More precisely, we can show the following regularity result.

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
\delta j^{\ell}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)
, for
\ell=1,2
. Then,
\mathsf{S}_{\ell}=\mathcal{T}_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)
. Moreover, the bound

(5.88)

\left\lVert\mathsf{S}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\lVert\delta j^{1}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\delta j^{2}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\right),\quad\ell=1,2,

holds true.

Proof.

First, similarly as in the proof of Corollary
5.15
, we notice that the multiplier associated with
\mathcal{T}_{0}^{-1}

is a Mikhlin-Hörmander multiplier in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of degree
1
plus a smoothing multiplier. Furthermore,
\mathcal{R}_{x},\mathcal{R}_{y}
are zero order operators with Mikhlin-Hörmander multipliers in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of degree
0
.
Thus,

(5.89)

\left\lVert\mathsf{S}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{1})\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{2})\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\right), \ \ell=1,2.

To conclude, we notice that the operator
\mathcal{A}
understood as above coincides with the Dirichlet to Neumann operator and therefore

\left\lVert\mathcal{A}(\delta j^{\ell})\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\lVert\delta j^{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)},\ \ell=1,2.

Combining both bounds we show the desired estimate.
∎

A straightforward consequence of Proposition
5.17
combined with the Hölder estimates for the transport problem collected in Corollary
2.17
reads as follows

Corollary 5.18
.

There exists
M_{0}
small enough so that for every
b\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq M<M_{0}
, there exists a constat
C=C(\alpha,L)
such that

\left\lVert\mathsf{S}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq CM\|j_{0}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}.

Moreover, we have that

\left\lVert\mathsf{S}_{\ell}[b_{1}]-\mathsf{S}_{\ell}[b_{2}]\right\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\leq C\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}.

To conclude this subsection, let us first show a result regarding Hölder estimates of the functions
\Lambda
and
\Theta
.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
M_{0}
be sufficiently small and let
b\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq M<M_{0}
. Moreover, define the functions

(5.90)

\Lambda(r,z)=\Psi_{z}(r,z)-r\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Theta(r,z)=|J_{g}(r,z)|-1.

Here,
\Psi_{z}
is the unique solution of the system of ODEs (
2.27
) with
v=b
and
J_{g}
denotes the determinant of the Jacobian of
\Psi_{z}
. The we have that

\Lambda\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega),\Theta\in C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)
and

(5.91)

\|\Lambda\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq CM,\quad\|\Theta\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq CM.

Moreover, for
b_{1},b_{2}\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|b_{1},b_{2}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq M<M_{0}
we have that

(5.92)

\displaystyle\|\Lambda[b_{1}]-\Lambda[b_{2}]\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}},

(5.93)

\displaystyle\|\Theta[b_{1}]-\Theta[b_{2}]\|_{C^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}}.

Proof.

The proof follows standard arguments to compute the dependence of the solution of an ODE in their parameters. More precisely, it should be notice that
\Lambda
basically satisfies the ODE (
2.27
) of Proposition
2.13
and therefore we can repeat the same ideas as the one developed there. In order to do so, we have to control incremental quotients combined with Grönwall type estimates. A bound similar to (
5.91
) is shown in
[
3
, Lemma 3.7]
in the two-dimensional case and for
C^{1,\alpha}
regularity instead of
C^{2,\alpha}
regularity. Moreover, in order to show (
5.92
)-(
5.93
) we calculate the ODE satisfied by the differences of the solutions which defined
\Lambda,\Theta
(cf. (
2.27
) and (
5.90
)) for
v=b_{1},v=b_{2}
.
∎

5.6.
Solution to the integral equation for
j_{0}

In this subsection we are interested in studying the existence of a solution
j_{0}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})
to the integral equation

\displaystyle j^{1}_{0}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{x}-\text{id})\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\widetilde{\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{1}}+\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{2}+\mathsf{S}_{2}[\widetilde{\delta j^{1}},\delta j^{2}]-\mathsf{H}_{2}+\left(\text{id}-\mathcal{R}_{x}^{2}\right)\widetilde{\textsf{G}_{2}}+\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\textsf{G}_{1}

(5.94)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\langle(\partial_{1}g_{2}-\partial_{2}g_{1})g_{1}\rangle-\langle j^{1}_{0}f\rangle,

\displaystyle j^{2}_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{y}-\text{id}\right)\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\widetilde{\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{2}}+\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j^{1}_{0}-\mathsf{S}_{1}[\delta j^{1},\widetilde{\delta j^{2}}]+\mathsf{H}_{1}-\left(\text{id}-\mathcal{R}_{y}^{2}\right)\widetilde{\textsf{G}_{1}}-\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\textsf{G}_{2}

(5.95)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\langle(\partial_{1}g_{2}-\partial_{2}g_{1})g_{2}\rangle-\langle j^{2}_{0}f\rangle.

Recall that in the previous subsections, namely in Subsection
5.2
-Subsection
5.5
, we have provided a rigorous approach to define all the operators contained in (
5.94
)-(
5.95
). Moreover, before solving the integral equation, it is convenient to introduce the modified operators
\mathsf{H}^{\star}_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
given by

(5.96)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}^{\star}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}_{1}-\mathcal{B}_{y}\left(b_{1}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}+b_{2}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0}-\partial_{3}b_{3}j^{3}_{0}\right),

(5.97)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}^{\star}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}_{2}-\mathcal{B}_{x}\left(b_{1}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}+b_{2}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0}-\partial_{3}b_{3}j^{3}_{0}\right).

The modified operators
\mathsf{H}^{\star}_{\ell}
do not depend on
j_{0}^{3}
which is already given in terms of the boundary data
g
. Indeed, recalling (
4.38
) we find that

j_{0}^{3}=\partial_{1}g_{2}-\partial_{2}g_{1},\ \mbox{on }\partial\Omega_{-}.

Hence, in order to solve the integral equation (
5.94
)-(
5.95
)
we introduce the following operator
\Upsilon_{\ell}:C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\longrightarrow C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,
\ell=1,2
given by

(5.98)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{1}(j^{1}_{0},j^{2}_{0})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{x}-\text{id})\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\widetilde{\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{1}}+\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{2}+\mathsf{S}_{2}[\widetilde{\delta j^{1}},\delta j^{2}]-\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\star}-\langle j^{1}_{0}f\rangle,

(5.99)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{2}(j^{1}_{0},j^{2}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{R}^{2}_{y}-\text{id}\right)\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\widetilde{\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{2}}+\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j^{1}_{0}-\mathsf{S}_{1}[\delta j^{1},\widetilde{\delta j^{2}}]+\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\star}-\langle j^{2}_{0}f\rangle.

Therefore, if
\Upsilon=(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})
, one could argue naively that the solution of the integral equations (
5.94
)-(
5.95
) should be given by

(5.100)

(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})=\left(\textbf{1}+\Upsilon\right)^{-1}\left(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2}\right),

where

(5.101)

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\text{id}-\mathcal{R}_{x}^{2}\right)\widetilde{\textsf{G}_{2}}+\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\textsf{G}_{1}-\langle(\partial_{1}g_{2}-\partial_{2}g_{1})g_{1}\rangle+\mathcal{B}_{x}\left(b_{1}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}+b_{2}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0}-\partial_{3}b_{3}j^{3}_{0}\right),

(5.102)

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\left(\text{id}-\mathcal{R}_{y}^{2}\right)\widetilde{\textsf{G}_{1}}-\mathcal{R}_{x}\mathcal{R}_{y}\textsf{G}_{2}-\langle(\partial_{1}g_{2}-\partial_{2}g_{1})g_{2}\rangle-\mathcal{B}_{y}\left(b_{1}\partial_{1}j^{3}_{0}+b_{2}\partial_{2}j^{3}_{0}-\partial_{3}b_{3}j^{3}_{0}\right).

Let us a provide a rigorous proof of the previous naively approach.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
M_{0}
be sufficiently small (in particular,
M_{0}\leq\displaystyle{\delta_{0},\delta_{1}}
) and let
b\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq M<M_{0}
.
Moreover, assume that

(5.103)

\max\left\{\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\,\|g\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\right\}\leq M.

Then
\left(\textbf{1}+\Upsilon\right)
is invertible in
C^{1,\alpha}
. More precisely, there exists an operator
\Pi=\left(\textbf{1}+\Upsilon\right)^{-1}
satisfying the bound

(5.104)

\left\lVert\Pi\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}.

Furthermore, for any two magnetic fields
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
satisfying (
5.103
) we have that

(5.105)

\|\Pi[b_{1}]-\Pi[b_{2}]\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}\leq C\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}.

Proof.

Recalling (
5.98
)-(
5.99
) and using the fact that the operators
\mathcal{R}_{x},\mathcal{R}_{y}
are Mikhlin-Hörmander mutipliers in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of degree
0
, we readily check that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Upsilon_{1}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\left\lVert\widetilde{\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{1}}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\left\lVert\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{2}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\mathsf{S}_{2}[\widetilde{\delta j^{1}},\delta j^{2}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\star}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\langle j^{1}_{0}f\rangle\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\right),

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Upsilon_{2}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\left\lVert\widetilde{\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{2}}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\sum_{\kappa=1}^{4}\left\lVert\textsf{T}_{\kappa}j_{0}^{1}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\mathsf{S}_{1}[\delta j^{1},\widetilde{\delta j^{2}}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\star}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\langle j^{2}_{0}f\rangle\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\right).

Applying estimates (
5.43
), (
5.83
) and (
5.88
) we find that for
\ell=1,2

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Upsilon_{{\ell}}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left((\|\Lambda\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\|\Theta\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}})(\left\lVert j_{0}^{1}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert j_{0}^{2}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}})+\left\lVert\delta j^{1}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\delta j^{1}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\right).

Moreover, using Corollary
5.9
, Proposition
5.19
and the smallness assumption (
5.103
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Upsilon_{{\ell}}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq CM(\left\lVert j_{0}^{1}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert j_{0}^{2}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}),\ \ell=1,2,

and thus
\|\Upsilon\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{1,\alpha})}
. Since
M
is small enough,
\|\Upsilon\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{1,\alpha})}<1
and hence, by means of a classical Neumann series argument (c.f.
[
4
,
34
]
), we conclude that
\textbf{1}+\Upsilon
is invertible. In addition, we also obtain the simple estimate

(5.106)

\left\lVert\Pi\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}=\left\lVert(\textbf{1}+\Upsilon)^{-1}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}=\left\lVert\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\Upsilon^{n}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq\frac{1}{1-CM}(\left\lVert j_{0}^{1}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert j_{0}^{2}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}).

Here, we have used that
\|\Upsilon\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{1,\alpha})}
. Moreover, for
n=0
, we have that
\Upsilon^{0}=\mathbf{1}
.
For the second assertion, we expand
\Pi=(\textbf{1}+\Upsilon)^{-1}
in its Neumann series expansion, namely,

\Pi[b_{1}]-\Pi[b_{2}]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\left(\Upsilon[b_{1}]^{n}-\Upsilon[b_{2}]^{n}\right)

Next, noting that
A^{n}-B^{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}A^{n-i}(A-B)B^{i-1}
we have that

\|A^{n}-B^{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}\leq\|A-B\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|A\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}^{n-i}\|B\|^{i-1}_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}.

As a result, we conclude that

\|\Pi[b_{1}]-\Pi[b_{2}]\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}\leq\|\Upsilon[b_{1}]-\Upsilon[b_{2}]\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\|\Upsilon[b_{1}]\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}+\|\Upsilon[b_{2}]\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}\right)^{n}.

Estimate (
5.105
) follows by recalling the precise expression of the operator
\Upsilon
given in (
5.98
)-(
5.99
) combined with smallness assumption (
5.103
), the difference estimates for
\textsf{T}^{d}_{\kappa}
,
\mathsf{S}_{\ell}
,
\mathsf{H}_{\ell}
and
\Lambda,\Theta
in Proposition
5.13
, Proposition
5.18
, Proposition
5.16
, Proposition
5.19
, respectively.
∎

Corollary 5.21
.

Let the hypothesis of Proposition
5.20
hold. Then there exists a solution
(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})\in C^{1,\alpha}
to (
5.100
) given by

(5.107)

(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})=\Pi\left(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2}\right),

with
\mathscr{G}_{1}
and
\mathscr{G}_{2}
given in (
5.101
) and (
5.102
), respectively. Furthermore,

(5.108)

\left\lVert(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C\left(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\right).

Proof.

The fact that
j_{0}
as given in (
5.107
) is a solution to (
5.100
) is a direct consequence of the definition of the operator
\Pi
in Proposition
5.20
. Moreover, recalling the multipliers associated to
\mathcal{R}_{x},\mathcal{R}_{y}
and
\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{B}_{y}
are Mikhlin-Hörmander multipliers in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of degree
0
and
-1
, respectively we find that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{G}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\lVert\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert\mathsf{G}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}+\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{2}_{C^{2,\alpha}}+\left\lVert b\right\rVert\left\lVert j_{0}^{3}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}\right),

(5.109)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}+\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{2}_{C^{2,\alpha}}+\left\lVert b\right\rVert\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\right),

where in the second inequality we have used estimate (
5.79
) of Proposition
5.15
and the fact that
j_{0}^{3}=\partial_{1}g_{2}-\partial_{2}g_{1},\ \mbox{on }\partial\Omega_{-}.
Therefore, this estimate combined with (
5.104
) shows bound (
5.108
).
∎

To conclude this section we outlining the reason why the solution of
(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})=\Pi(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})
yields the unique choice of
(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})
that ensures that the new magnetic field
W
satisfies the correct boundary conditions. Note that in Theorem
2.12
, we proved the existence and uniqueness of the div-curl problem given
j
divergence-free,
f\in C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)
and
J_{1},J_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. In such construction, we employed a combination of a scalar and a vector potential for the magnetic field, while in Section
3
and
4
we employed an a priori different construction. In the following, we show why this solution is the unique solution of the div-curl system, by using the uniqueness result we showed in Proposition
2.12
. To that end, we take the vector field
A
defined as the unique solution for the following elliptic problem

(5.110)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}-\Delta A=j&\text{ in }\Omega\\
A_{1}=h_{1}&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\\
A_{2}=h_{2}&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\\
\partial_{3}A_{3}=-\partial_{1}A_{1}-\partial_{2}A_{2}&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\end{array}\right.,

where
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are defined in
\partial\Omega_{-}
and
\partial\Omega_{+}
in (
4.6
). Notice that the functions
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
only gain extra regularity in one of the variables, so we cannot expect to directly obtain
C^{3,\alpha}
bounds for
A
. However, we can separate (
5.110
) into

(5.111)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}-\Delta A^{1}=0&\text{ in }\Omega\\
A^{1}_{1}=h_{1}&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\\
A^{1}_{2}=h_{2}&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\\
\partial_{3}A^{1}_{3}=-\partial_{1}A_{1}-\partial_{2}A_{2}&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\end{array}\right.,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}-\Delta A^{2}=j&\text{ in }\Omega\\
A^{2}_{1}=0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\\
A^{2}_{2}=0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\\
\partial_{3}A^{2}_{3}=0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\end{array}\right..

The left-hand side problem yields an harmonic
A^{1}
function which can be extended to a
C^{2,\alpha}
function in
\Omega
. Notice that, since it is harmonic in the interior, it must be smooth. On the other hand
A^{2}
is clearly in
C^{3,\alpha}
. The resulting
A
needs to have zero divergence. Indeed, since
A
is
C^{3}
in the interior of our domain, we can permute the Laplacian with the divergence, and conclude that
\text{div}\,A
is harmonic and with zero boundary values. Thus, it is zero. Therefore, and since the maps
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are constructed to this end, we conclude that
\nabla\times A
yields the unique solution for (
2.16
). Now, once we have seen that
\nabla\times A
yields the unique
C^{2,\alpha}
solution of the div-curl system, we can take Fourier coefficients to yield the relations that
(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})
need to satisfy which, together with the divergence free condition, are equivalent to the integral equations (
5.94
)-(
5.95
), whose existence and uniqueness of solutions have been proved in the previous result, cf. Corollary
5.21
.

6.
The fixed point argument and proof of Theorem
1.1

In this final section, we will provide the fixed point argument, this is, we will defined an adequate operator
\Gamma
which has a fixed point such that
B=(0,0,1)+b
is a solution satisfying the boundary value problem (
1.9
), thus proving Theorem
1.1
.

So far, we have constructed a map that takes an initial perturbation
b\in C^{2,\alpha}
, and maps it to a current
j=\Pi[b]\in C^{1,\alpha}
so that the corresponding solution of the div-curl system,

(6.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\nabla\times W=j&\text{ in }\Omega\\
\text{div}\,W=0&\text{ in }\Omega\\
W\cdot n=f&\text{ on }\partial\Omega\\
\end{array}\right.\,\text{ and }\,\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{\{x=0\}}W\cdot d\vec{S}=J_{1}[j_{0},f,g,b]\\
\int_{\{y=0\}}W\cdot d\vec{S}=J_{2}[j_{0},f,g,b]\end{array}\right.,

satisfies (formally) the boundary condition
W_{\tau}=g
. Denote
\mathscr{B}[J,f,j]=W[b,j]
such solution. In this section we will prove that this resulting map

(6.2)

b\mapsto\Gamma[b]=\mathscr{B}\left[J,f,\Pi[b]\right]

attains a unique fixed point in an adequate space, that corresponds to the solution of our problem.

Due to Proposition
2.12
, the problem (
6.1
) is well-posed in
C^{2,\alpha}
, so that the map
\Gamma
is well defined. Next, we will use all the previous results we have obtained in the previous section to prove that (
6.2
) admits a unique fixed point in some particular subset of
C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega)
. This fixed point will be a solution to our problem.

6.1.
Fixed point for the map
\Gamma

In order to prove that a fixed point for the map
\Gamma
exists, we must make precise where such fixed point is attained, as well as a precise description on how the map
\Gamma
is constructed. We begin with this second task.

First of all, we need to fix a (small)
M>0
, so that
M<M_{0}
, and
M_{0}
is given by Proposition
2.15
. Then, given an initial perturbation
b\in C^{2,\alpha}
satisfying
\|b\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq M
, we can construct a map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}T[b]:C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})&\longrightarrow&C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\\
(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})&\mapsto&(j^{1},j^{2},j^{3})\end{array},

where
j=(j^{1},j^{2},j^{3})
is the unique solution for the equation (
2.29
) with initial condition
j_{0}=(j^{1}_{0},j^{2}_{0},j_{0}^{3})
, and
j_{0}^{3}=\partial_{2}g_{1}-\partial_{1}g_{2}
. Due to Proposition
2.15
, this map is well defined and is bounded.

Now, after making
M
smaller, as in Proposition
5.20
, if we have boundary data
f\in C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)
and
g\in C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})
satisfying
\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)},\|g\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})}\leq M
, we can take
(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})
to be equal to

(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})=\Pi[b](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})=\left(\textbf{1}+\Upsilon[b]\right)^{-1}(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2}),

where
\mathscr{G}_{1}
and
\mathscr{G}_{2}
are given by (
5.101
) and (
5.102
). Note that these functions are well defined and belong to
C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, so the formula for
(j_{0}^{1},j_{0}^{2})
is well defined.

It is worth noticing that the resulting current
j=T[b](\Pi[b](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2}))
is divergence free by construction. Therefore, we can solve the div-curl problem (
2.16
), where we take
(J_{1},J_{2})
as in (
6.4
)-(
6.5
). We then conclude that the map
\Gamma
is given by the following composition

\Gamma[b]=\mathscr{B}\left[f,J,T[b]\left(\Pi[b](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})\right)\right].

We next prove that, in some subspace of
C^{1,\alpha}
, this operator admits a fixed point.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
f\in C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)
satisfying the integrability condition (
1.8
) and
g\in C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})
. There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
M=M(L,\alpha)
small enough such that if

\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)}+\|g\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})}\leq M\varepsilon_{0},

then
\Gamma
maps
B_{M}(C^{2,\alpha})
into itself. Furthermore, the operator
\Gamma
admits a unique fixed point in
B_{M}(C^{2,\alpha})
.

Proof.

For the first step, we use that all of the operators involved in the definition of
\Gamma
satisfy suitable bounds, so that

\begin{split}\|\Gamma[b]\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)}&\leq C\left(\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)}+|J|+\|T[b]\left(\Pi[b](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})\right)\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)}+|J|+\|g\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})}+\|\Pi[b](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)}+\|g\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-})}\right)\leq CM\epsilon_{0}.\end{split}

In the first line, we have used the estimates for
\mathscr{B}
from Theorem
2.12
, in the second line we have used boundedness of the operator
T[b]
, which arises from Theorem
2.15
, and the last line comes from writing the explicit expresion for
J
, the fact that
\|\Pi\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}
is bounded, and the bounds for the
\mathscr{G}_{\ell}
defined in (
5.101
) and (
5.102
). Thus, if we take
\varepsilon_{0}<1/(C+1)
, we get that
\Gamma
maps
B_{M}(C^{2,\alpha})
into itself.

In order to prove that a fixed point is attained, we use Banach’s fixed point. However, we will not equip
B_{M}(C^{2,\alpha})
with the
C^{2,\alpha}
norm, but with the
C^{1,\alpha}
one. The reason behind this is that all the estimates we have made in terms of differences are given in terms of
C^{1,\alpha}
norms. We can use Banach’s fixed point theorem, since by Arzelà-Ascoli the closed balls of
C^{2,\alpha}
are compact in
C^{1,\alpha}
, c.f.
[
4
]
. Then, we can see that

\begin{split}\|\Gamma[b_{1}]-\Gamma[b_{2}]\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}&\leq C\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\\
&=\|\mathscr{B}[f,J^{1},T[b_{1}]\left(\Pi[b_{1}](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})\right)]-\mathscr{B}[f,J^{2},T[b_{2}]\left(\Pi[b_{2}](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})\right)]\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\\
&\leq C\left(|J^{1}-J^{2}|+\|T[b_{1}]\left(\Pi[b_{1}](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})\right)-T[b_{2}]\left(\Pi[b_{2}](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})\right)\|_{C^{\alpha}(\Omega)}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(|J^{1}-J^{2}|+\|(\Pi[b_{1}]-\Pi[b_{2}])(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})\|_{C^{\alpha}}+\|\Pi[b_{1}](\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2})\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\right).\end{split}

Invoking Proposition
5.20
, we have that
\|\Pi[b_{1}]-\Pi[b_{2}]\|_{\mathcal{L}(C^{\alpha})}\leq C\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}
. Therefore,

\|\Gamma[b_{1}]-\Gamma[b_{2}]\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)}+\|g\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\partial\Omega_{-}}\right)\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq CM\varepsilon_{0}\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}

Taking
\varepsilon_{0}\leq 1/(CM+1)
, we conclude that
\Gamma
is a contraction mapping in the complete metric space
X=(B_{M}(C^{2,\alpha}),\|\cdot\|_{C^{1,\alpha}})
, so by Banach’s fixed point, we conclude that it admits a unique fixed point.
∎

6.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

During this work, we have constructed a map
\Gamma
which attains a unique fixed point
b
in the metric space
(B_{M}(C^{2,\alpha}),\|\cdot\|_{C^{1,\alpha}})
that solves the div-curl problem (
6.1
). The last step remaining to prove Theorem
1.1
is checking that this fixed point is indeed the solution for (
1.1
) with our set of boundary conditions. Notice that, by construction,
B=(0,0,1)+b
satisfies the normal boundary condition, namely,
B\cdot n=1+f
with
n
being the outer normal vector in
\partial\Omega_{+}
and the inner normal vector in
\partial\Omega_{-}
. Furthermore, since in the very definition of
\Gamma
we have ensured that
\Gamma[b]
satisfies the tangential boundary condition (c.f. check the last digression in Section
5
), we just need to check that there exists a unique well defined pressure function
p\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)
such that

j\times B=\nabla p,\ \mbox{in }\Omega,

with
j=\nabla\times B
.
One might think that the pressure can be written simply as

(6.3)

p=\int_{0}^{\textbf{{x}}}j\times B,\ \mbox{in }\Omega,

where the integral is taken between any curve that connects
(0,0,0)
and
\textbf{{x}}=(x,y,z)
. However, for this to make sense, we need to ensure the following two conditions:

•

There cannot be any dependence on the chosen curve. At least near zero, this is achieved only if
j\times B
has zero curl. This is indeed the case, since

\nabla\times(j\times B)=(B\cdot\nabla)-j(\nabla\cdot A)+j(\nabla\cdot B)-(j\cdot\nabla)B.

As
j
and
B
are divergence free by construction, the last two items vanish. On the other hand, since
b
is a fixed point of
\Gamma
, it satisfies
(j\cdot\nabla)B-(B\cdot\nabla)j=0
, so we get the result.

•

In our domain the points
(x,y,z)
and
(x+2\pi l_{1},y+2\pi l_{2},z)
both represent the same point for every
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, in order for
p
to be a well defined function in
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,L]
, we need

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(j\times B\right)_{1}(x,y,0)\ dx=0,\ \forall\,y\in\mathbb{R},\quad\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(j\times B\right)_{2}(x,y,0)\,dy=0,\ \forall\,x\in\mathbb{R}.

Using the fact that
\nabla\times(j\times B)=0
and the periodicity of the integrand, we infer that the integrals above are constant functions of
y
and
x
, respectively. Hence, we can then integrate on the other variable, so that these two conditions are equivalent to

(6.4)

\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(j\times B\right)_{1}(x,y,0)\ dxdy=\langle j_{0}^{2}\rangle+\langle j_{0}^{2}f\rangle-\left\langle\left(\partial_{1}g_{2}-\partial_{2}g_{1}\right)g_{2}\right\rangle=0,

(6.5)

\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(j\times B\right)_{2}(x,y,0)\ dxdy=-\langle j_{0}^{1}\rangle-\langle j_{0}^{1}f\rangle+\left\langle\left(\partial_{1}g_{2}-\partial_{2}g_{1}\right)g_{1}\right\rangle=0.

Note that these equations are satisfied due to our particular tailor-made choice of
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
in
\eqref{J1}-\eqref{J2}
. This also justifies the selection of the constants
J_{1},J_{2}
, which might have looked unjustified and arbitrary at the beginning.

Summarizing, we have defined a solution pair
(B,p)\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)
that satisfies the MHS equations with Grad-Rubin conditions, i.e., system (
1.9
). Furthermore, due to our construction, and the uniqueness granted by the Banach’s fixed point, we conclude that the solutions is the only one satisfying

\|B-(0,0,1)\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq M.

Thus, Theorem
1.1
follows.

Appendix A
Complementary results Besov spaces and pseudo-differential operators

In this appendix we include for the sake of completeness a detailed proof of Theorem
2.5
which can be traced back to
[
30
, Lemma 4.5]
.

A.1.
Proof of Theorem
2.5

The main step of this proof is based on a delicate decomposition of
a
into building blocks of the form
a_{jk}=\widecheck{\varphi}_{j}*a(\cdot,\xi)\varphi_{k}(\xi)
. Note that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the new symbols
a_{jk}
are
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the
\xi
variable. Therefore, the inverse Fourier Transform with respect to
\xi
yields a kernel
K_{jk}(x,z)
which is integrable with respect to the
z
variable. Indeed,

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|K_{jk}(x,z)|dz&=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a_{jk}(x,\xi)e^{i\xi\cdot z}\right|dz\\
&=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{(1+|z|^{2})}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a_{jk}(x,\xi)\left(1-\Delta_{\xi}\right)e^{i\xi\cdot z}d\xi\right|dz\\
&=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{(1+|z|^{2})}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(1-\Delta_{\xi}\right)a_{jk}(x,\xi)e^{i\xi\cdot z}d\xi\right|dz.\end{split}

Iterating this procedure, we find that
K_{jk}
is integrable with respect to the
z
variable. Moreover,
\text{Op}(a_{jk})u=\text{Op}(a_{jk})(u_{k-1}+u_{k}+u_{k+1})
, with
u_{k}=\varphi_{k}(D)u
. This justifies the use of the dyadic partition of unity in the
\xi
variable, as we inmediately obtain information about the
u_{k}
, which are the main ingredients of the Besov norm.

Next, we compute the decay of
z^{\alpha}K_{jk}
by usual estimates that involve the Fourier Transform. We see that, since the
\varphi_{j}
are supported in an annulus, then
\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{j}(0)=0
for any multiindex
\alpha
, which means that the integral of
x^{\alpha}\widecheck{\varphi}
vanishes for every
\alpha
. By means of Taylor’s theorem, we have that for every
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there is some
c\in[-y,0]
such that

\partial^{\gamma}_{\xi}a(x-y,\xi)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq\lfloor s\rfloor}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial^{\gamma}_{\xi}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a(x,\xi)(-y)^{\alpha}+\sum_{|\alpha|=\lfloor s\rfloor}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial^{\gamma}_{\xi}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a(x+c,\xi)(-y)^{\alpha}.

Therefore, convoluting the previous equality agains
\widecheck{\varphi}
, we have the bound

\begin{split}|\widecheck{\varphi}_{j}*\partial^{\gamma}_{\xi}a(\cdot,\xi)|&\leq\sum_{|\alpha|=\lfloor s\rfloor}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\|\partial^{\gamma}_{\xi}a(\cdot,\xi)\|_{C^{s}}\int|y|^{s}|2^{jd}\widecheck{\varphi}\left(2^{j}y\right)dy\\
&\leq 2^{-js}\|\partial^{\gamma}_{\xi}a(\cdot,\xi)\|_{C^{s}}\sum_{|\alpha|=\lfloor s\rfloor}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|y|^{s}|\widecheck{\varphi}\left(y\right)|dy.\end{split}

By means of integration by parts we find that

\displaystyle z^{\alpha}K_{jk}(x,z)=\frac{z^{\alpha}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widecheck{\varphi}_{j}*a(\cdot,\xi)\varphi_{k}(\xi)e^{i\xi\cdot z}d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widecheck{\varphi}_{j}*a(\cdot,\xi)\varphi_{k}(\xi)\frac{\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}}{i^{\alpha}}e^{i\xi\cdot z}d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{i^{\alpha}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\left[\widecheck{\varphi}_{j}*a(\cdot,\xi)\varphi_{k}(\xi)\right]e^{i\xi\cdot z}d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{i^{\alpha}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}{\alpha\choose\gamma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widecheck{\varphi}_{j}*\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a(\cdot,\xi)\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha-\beta}\varphi_{k}(\xi)e^{i\xi\cdot z}d\xi.

Hence, using the fact that
\varphi_{k}
is supported in the ball of radius
2^{k}
, and
\varphi_{k}=\varphi(2^{-k}\cdot)
, we invoke the decay if
\|\partial^{\gamma}_{\xi}a(\cdot,\xi)\|_{C^{s}}
to obtain the estimate

\displaystyle|z^{\alpha}K_{jk}(x,z)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}{\alpha\choose\gamma}\|\widecheck{\varphi}_{j}*\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a(\cdot,\xi)\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha-\beta}\varphi_{k}\|_{L^{1}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\leq\frac{C_{\alpha,s}}{\alpha!}\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}{\alpha\choose\gamma}2^{-js}2^{-k|\alpha-\beta|}\int_{3/4\cdot 2^{k-1}<|\xi|<8/3\cdot 2^{k+1}}(1+|\xi|)^{m-|\beta|}d\xi\leq C\cdot\|a\|_{m,s,0}\cdot 2^{-js+mk+dk-k|\alpha|},

where the constant
C
does not depend on
a
nor on
j
,
k
or
x
. Next, fix
|\alpha|>n
and define the function

\omega_{k}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2^{-kd},&|y|\leq 2^{-k}\\
2^{-kd+2|\alpha|}|y|^{2|\alpha|},&|y|\geq 2^{-k}\end{array}\right..

Due to the above computations, we have that

\|\omega_{k}K_{jk}\|_{\infty}\leq C\cdot\|a\|_{m,s,2|\alpha|}\cdot 2^{-js+km}.

Furthermore, noticing that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{\omega_{k}}dy<C,

with
C
independent of
j
and
k
, leads to the estimate

\displaystyle\|\text{Op}(a_{jk})u\|_{L^{1}}=\|\text{Op}(a_{jk})u_{k}\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{jk}(x,x-z)u(z)dz\right|dx

\displaystyle\leq\|\omega_{k}K_{jk}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{\omega_{k}}dy\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u_{k}(y)|dy\leq C\cdot\|a\|_{m,s,2|\alpha|}\cdot 2^{-js+km}\|u_{k}\|_{L^{1}}.

We have derived proper estimates on the norms of
\text{Op}(a)
, so we can divide
a
into adequate combinations of these building blocks. Notice that the convolution with
\widecheck{\varphi}_{j}
gives us the contribution of the Fourier modes (with respect to the first variable) contained in the annulus
2^{j-1}<|\zeta|<2^{j+1}
. Thus, it is convenient to divide
a
in pieces where such Fourier modes are smaller than
\xi
and greater than
\xi
, namely,

a=\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=-1}^{k-5}a_{jk}+\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k-4}^{\infty}a_{jk}=a_{1}+a_{2}.

Taking
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, let use separate the different computations for
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
.

Estimates for
\text{Op}(a_{1})

This is the simplest case, as the spectrum of
a_{1}
is well controlled by
\xi
. To that purpose, we consider the symbol
b_{1k}=\sum_{j=-1}^{k-5}a_{jk}
. Then, by the same arguments as above, the kernel
K_{k}
satisfies

\|K_{k}w_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\cdot\|a\|_{m,s,2|\alpha|}\cdot 2^{km}\Longrightarrow\|\text{Op}(b_{1k})u\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\cdot\|a\|_{m,s,2|\alpha|}\cdot 2^{km}\|u_{k}\|_{L^{1}}.

Making use of the fact that the spectrum of
\text{Op}(b_{1k})u
is well controlled due to the construction of the building blocks, we find testing againts a given Schwartz function
v
that

\displaystyle\langle\hbox{\hskip \displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b_{1k}(x,\xi)\hbox{\hskip \text{Op}(b_{1k})u
has a Fourier transform which is supported in an annulus given by
C2^{k}\leq|\xi|\leq C2^{k+1}
, where
C
is independent of
u
or
k
. Moreover,

\begin{split}\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}2^{-ks}\|\text{Op}(b_{1k})u\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\|a\|_{m,s,2|\alpha|}\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}\sum_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 1}2^{-k(s-m)}\|u_{k^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}}&\leq C\|a\|_{m,s,2|\alpha|}\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}2^{k(m-s)}\|u_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\\
&=C\|a\|_{m,s,2|\alpha|}\|u\|_{B^{(m-s)_{1,1}}}.\end{split}

Estimates for
\text{Op}(a_{2})

Here we can use our previous computations directly, so that

\displaystyle\|\text{Op}(a_{2})u\|_{L^{1}}\leq\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k-4}^{\infty}\|\text{Op}(a_{jk})u\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\|a\|_{m,s,2|\alpha|}\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k-4}^{\infty}2^{-js+km}\|u_{k}\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\|a\|_{m,s,2|\alpha|}\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{k(m-s)}\|u_{k}\|_{L^{1}}.

Therefore, due to the density of Schwartz functions, and the fact that
L^{1}
embeds continuously into
B^{-s}_{1,1}
, the result follows.
